Asynchronous Probabilistic Couplings in Higher-Order Separation Logic DOI: 10.1145/3632868 popl24main-p129-p Journal: PACMPL Volume: 8 POPL 26 1 CCS: Theory of computation Separation logic CCS: Theory of computation Logic and verification CCS: Theory of computation Probabilistic computation CCS: Theory of computation Program verification CCS: Mathematics of computing Probabilistic algorithms
Simon Oddershede Gregersen, Alejandro Aguirre, Philipp G. Haselwarter, Joseph Tassarotti, Lars Birkedal

Abstract
. Probabilistic couplings are the foundation for many probabilistic relational program logics and arise when relating random sampling statements across two programs. In relational program logics, this manifests as dedicated coupling rules that, e.g. , say we may reason as if two sampling statements return the same value. However, this approach fundamentally requires aligning or “synchronizing” the sampling statements of the two programs which is not always possible. In this paper, we develop Clutch, a higher-order probabilistic relational separation logic that addresses this issue by supporting asynchronous probabilistic couplings. We use Clutch to develop a logical step-indexed logical relation to reason about contextual refinement and equivalence of higher-order programs written in a rich language with a probabilistic choice operator, higher-order local state, and impredicative polymorphism. Finally, we demonstrate our approach on a number of case studies. All the results that appear in the paper have been formalized in the Coq proof assistant using the Coquelicot library and the Iris separation logic framework.



1.
Introduction

Relational reasoning is a useful technique for proving properties of probabilistic programs.
By relating a complex probabilistic program to a simpler one, we can often reduce a challenging verification task to an easier one.
In addition, certain important properties of probabilistic programs are naturally expressed in a relational form, such as stability of machine learning algorithms
(
Bousquet and Elisseeff 2002
)
, differential privacy
(
Dwork and Roth 2013
)
, and provable security
(
Goldwasser and Micali 1984
)
.
Consequently, a number of relational program logics and models have been developed for probabilistic programs,
e.g.
, pRHL
(
Barthe et al. 2015
)
, approximate pRHL
(
Barthe et al. 2012
;
Barthe et al. 2016a
;
Barthe et al. 2016b
)
, EpRHL
(
Barthe et al. 2018
)
, HO-RPL
(
Aguirre et al. 2021
)
, Polaris
(
Tassarotti and Harper 2019
)
, logical relations
(
Bizjak and Birkedal 2015
;
Wand et al. 2018
;
Johann et al. 2010
)
, and differential logical relations
(
Dal Lago and Gavazzo 2022
)
.

Many probabilistic relational program logics make use of
probabilistic couplings

(
Thorisson 2000
;
Lindvall 2002
;
Villani 2008
)
, a mathematical tool for reasoning about pairs of probabilistic processes.
Informally, couplings correlate outputs of two processes by specifying how corresponding sampling statements are correlated.
To understand how couplings work in such logics, let us consider a pRHL-like logic.
In pRHL and its variants, we prove Hoare
quadruples
of the form
{\left\{P\right\}}\penalty\thinspace e_{1}\sim e_{2}\penalty\thinspace{\left\{Q\right\}}
, where
e_{1}
and
e_{2}
are two probabilistic programs,
and
P
and
Q
are pre and post-
relations
on states of the two programs.
Couplings arise when reasoning about random sampling statements in the two programs, such as in the following rule:

\frac{}{{\left\{P[v/x_{1},v/x_{2}]\right\}}\penalty\thinspace x_{1}\xleftarrow{\$}d\sim x_{2}\xleftarrow{\$}d\penalty\thinspace{\left\{P\right\}}}\quad\textsc{lab={prhl-couple}}

Here, the two programs both sample from the same distribution
d
and store the result in variable
x_{1}
and
x_{2}
, respectively.
The rule says that we may reason
as if
the two sampling statements return the same value
v
in both programs, and one says
that the sample statements have been “coupled”.
This is a powerful method that integrates well with existing reasoning principles from relational program logics.
However, this kind of coupling rules require aligning or “synchronizing” the sampling statements of the two programs: both programs have to be executing the sample statements we want to couple for their next step when applying the rule.
To enable this alignment, pRHL has various rules that enable taking steps on one side of the quadruple at a time or commuting statements in a (first-order) program.
Nevertheless, with the rules from existing probabilistic relational logics, it is not always possible to synchronize sampling statements.

For example, consider the following program written in an ML-like language that
eagerly
performs a probabilistic coin flip and returns the result in a thunk:

\displaystyle\mathit{eager}\triangleq{}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{let}}\penalty\thinspace b\mathrel{=}\operatorname{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{flip}}}()\penalty\thinspace\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{in}}\penalty\thinspace\lambda\_.\penalty\thinspace b

An indistinguishable—but
lazy
—version of the program only does the coin flip when the thunk is invoked for the first time but stores the result in a reference that is read from in future invocations:

\displaystyle\mathit{lazy}\triangleq{}

\displaystyle\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{let}}\penalty\thinspace r\mathrel{=}\operatorname{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{ref}}}(\operatorname{\mathsf{None}})\penalty\thinspace\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{in}}\penalty\thinspace

\displaystyle\lambda\_.\penalty\thinspace{\begin{array}[t]{rll}\lx@intercol\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{match}}\penalty\thinspace\mathop{!}r\penalty\thinspace\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{with}}\hfil\lx@intercol\\
&\operatorname{\mathsf{Some}}(b)&\Rightarrow b\\
|&\operatorname{\mathsf{None}}&\Rightarrow{\begin{array}[t]{l}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{let}}\penalty\thinspace b\mathrel{=}\operatorname{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{flip}}}()\penalty\thinspace\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{in}}\\
r\leftarrow\operatorname{\mathsf{Some}}(b);\\
b\end{array}}\\
\lx@intercol\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{end}}\hfil\lx@intercol\end{array}}

The usual symbolic execution rules of relational logics will allow us to progress the two sides independently according to the program execution, but they will
not
allow us to line up the
\operatorname{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{flip}}}()
expression in
\mathit{eager}
with that in
\mathit{lazy}
.
Consequently, the coupling rule
1
cannot be applied.
Intuitively, the
\operatorname{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{flip}}}()
expression in
\mathit{eager}
is evaluated immediately but the
\operatorname{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{flip}}}()
expression in
\mathit{lazy}
only gets evaluated when the thunk is invoked—to relate the two thunks one is forced to first evaluate the eager sampling, but this then makes it impossible to couple it with the lazy sampling.

While the example may seem contrived, these kinds of transformations of eager and lazy sampling are widely used,
e.g.
, in proofs in the Random Oracle Model
(
Bellare and Rogaway 1993
)
and in game playing proofs
(
Bellare and Rogaway 2004
;
Bellare and Rogaway 2006
)
.
For this reason, systems like EasyCrypt
(
Barthe et al. 2013
)
and CertiCrypt
(
Barthe et al. 2010
;
Barthe et al. 2009
)
support reasoning about lazy/eager sampling through special-purpose rules for swapping statements that allows alignment of samplings; the approach is shown to work for a first-order language with global state and relies on syntactic criteria and assertions on memory disjointness.
However, in rich enough languages (
e.g.
with general references and closures) these kinds of swapping-equivalences are themselves highly non-trivial, even in the non-probabilistic case
(
Pitts and Stark 1998
;
Dreyer et al. 2012
)
.

In this paper we develop
Clutch
, a higher-order probabilistic relational separation logic that addresses this issue by enabling
asynchronous
probabilistic couplings.
To do so, Clutch introduces a novel kind of ghost state, called
presampling tapes
.
Presampling tapes let us reason about sampling statements as if they executed ahead of time and stored their results for later use.
This converts the usual alignment problem of coupling rules into the task of reasoning about this special form of state.
Fortunately, reasoning about state is well-addressed with modern separation logics.

Clutch provides a “logical” step-indexed logical relation
(
Dreyer et al. 2011
)
to reason about
contextual refinement and equivalence
of probabilistic higher-order programs written in
\mathbf{F}_{\mu,\textnormal{ref}}^{\textnormal{rand}}{}
, a rich language with a probabilistic choice operator, higher-order local state, recursive types, and impredicative polymorphism.
Intuitively, expressions
e_{1}
and
e_{2}
of type
\tau
are contextually equivalent if no well-typed context
\mathcal{C}
can distinguish them,
i.e.
, if the expression
\mathcal{C}{[}e_{1}{]}
has the same observable behaviors as
\mathcal{C}{[}e_{2}{]}
.
Contextual equivalence can be decomposed into contextual refinement: we say
e_{1}
refines
e_{2}
at type
\tau
, written
e_{1}\precsim_{\text{ctx}}e_{2}:\tau
, if, for all contexts
\mathcal{C}
expecting something of type
\tau
, if
\mathcal{C}{[}e_{1}{]}
has some observable behavior, then so does
\mathcal{C}{[}e_{2}{]}
.
As our language is probabilistic, here “observable behavior” means the
probability
of observing an outcome, such as termination.
Using the
logical approach

(
Timany et al. 2022
)
, in Clutch, types are interpreted as relations expressed in separation logic.
The resulting model allows us to prove, among other examples, that the
\mathit{eager}
program above is contextually equivalent to the
\mathit{lazy}
program.

The work presented in this paper is
foundational

(
Appel 2001
)
in the sense that all results, including the semantics, the logic, the necessary mathematical analysis results, the relational model, and all the examples are formalized
1
1

1

https://github.com/logsem/clutch
in the Coq proof assistant
(
The Coq Development Team 2022
)
using the Coquelicot library
(
Boldo et al. 2015
)
and the Iris separation logic framework
(
Jung et al. 2015
;
Jung et al. 2016
;
Krebbers et al. 2017a
;
Jung et al. 2018
)
.

In summary, we make the following contributions:

•

A higher-order probabilistic relational separation logic, Clutch, for reasoning about probabilistic programs written in
\mathbf{F}_{\mu,\textnormal{ref}}^{\textnormal{rand}}
, an ML-like programming language with higher-order local state, recursive types, and impredicative polymorphism.

•

A proof method for relating asynchronous probabilistic samplings in a program logic; a methodology that allows us to reason about sampling as if it were state and to exploit existing separation logic mechanisms such as
ghost state
and
invariants
to reason about probabilistic programs.
We demonstrate the usefulness of the approach with a number of case studies.

•

The first coupling-based relational program logic to reason about contextual refinement and equivalence of programs in a higher-order language with local state, recursive types, and impredicative polymorphism.

•

Novel technical ideas, namely,
left-partial couplings
, a
coupling modality
, and an
erasure
argument, that allow us to prove soundness of the relational logic.

•

Full mechanization in Coq using Coquelicot and the Iris separation logic framework.

2.
Key Ideas

The key conceptual novelties of the Clutch logic are twofold: a
logical probabilistic refinement judgment
and a novel kind of ghost resource, called
presampling tapes
.

Logical refinement.

The refinement judgment
\Delta\vDash_{\mathcal{E}}e_{1}\precsim e_{2}:\tau
should be read as “the expression
e_{1}
refines the expression
e_{2}
at type
\tau
” and it satisfies a range of structural and symbolic execution rules as showcased in
Figure

21
and further explained in
Section

4
.
Just like contextual refinement, the judgment is indexed by a type
\tau
—the environment
\Delta
assigns semantic interpretations to type variables in
\tau
and
\mathcal{E}
is an
invariant mask
as elaborated on in
Section

4
.
Both are safely ignored in this section.
The meaning of the judgment is formally reflected by the following soundness theorem.

Theorem 1 (Soundness).

If
\emptyset\vDash e_{1}\precsim e_{2}:\tau
is derivable in Clutch then
e_{1}\precsim_{\text{ctx}}e_{2}:\tau
.

The refinement judgment is
internal
to the ambient Clutch separation logic.
This means that we can combine the judgment in arbitrary ways with other logical connectives:
e.g.
, the
separating conjunction

P\mskip 5.0mu{\ast}\mskip 5.0muQ
and its adjoint
separating implication
(magic wand)
P\mskip 1.66667mu\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\hbox to10.29pt{\vbox to4.65pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25833pt\lower-2.32639pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}{{}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@roundcap\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 10.43 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{5.0347pt}{-2.32639pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{62}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}}}\mskip 1.66667muQ
.
All inference rules that we present can be internalized as propositions in the logic and we will use an inference rule with premises
P_{1},\ldots,P_{n}
and conclusion
Q
as notation for
(P_{1}\mskip 5.0mu{\ast}\mskip 5.0mu\ldots\mskip 5.0mu{\ast}\mskip 5.0muP_{n})\vdash Q
.

The language
\mathbf{F}_{\mu,\textnormal{ref}}^{\textnormal{rand}}
contains a single probabilistic primitive
\operatorname{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{rand}}}(N)
that reduces uniformly at random to some
n\in\{0,1,\ldots,N\}
:

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{rand}}}(N),\sigma

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{{}}^{1/(N+1)}n,\sigma

\displaystyle n\in\{0,1,\ldots,N\}

where
\sigma
is the current program state and
\xrightarrow{{}}\subseteq\kern-0.50003pt\textit{Cfg}\times[0,1]\times\kern-0.50003pt\textit{Cfg}
is a small-step transition relation, annotated with the probability that the transition occurs.
By defining
\operatorname{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{flip}}}()\triangleq{}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{if}}\penalty\thinspace\operatorname{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{rand}}}(1)=0\penalty\thinspace\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{then}}\penalty\thinspace\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{false}}\penalty\thinspace\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{else}}\penalty\thinspace\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{true}}
we recover the Boolean fair coin flip operator used in the motivating example.
To reason relationally about probabilistic choices that
can
be synchronized, Clutch admits a classical coupling rule that allows us to continue reasoning as if the two sampled values are related by a bijection
f
on the sampling space
\{0,\dots,N\}
:

\displaystyle\frac{f\penalty\ \text{bijection}\quad\forall n\leq N.\penalty\thinspace\Delta\vDash_{\mathcal{E}}K{[}n{]}\precsim K^{\prime}{[}f(n){]}:\tau}{\Delta\vDash_{\mathcal{E}}K{[}\operatorname{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{rand}}}(N){]}\precsim K^{\prime}{[}\operatorname{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{rand}}}(N){]}:\tau}\quad\textsc{lab={rel-couple-rands}}{}

where
K
and
K^{\prime}
are arbitrary evaluation contexts.

Asynchronous couplings.

To support
asynchronous
couplings we introduce
presampling tapes
.
Reminiscent of how
prophecy variables

(
Abadi and Lamport 1988
;
Abadi and Lamport 1991
;
Jung et al. 2020
)
allow us to talk about the future, presampling tapes give us the means to talk about the outcome of probabilistic choices
in the future
.
2
2

2

As showcased in
Section

7
, however, prophecy variables as previously developed in Iris are unsound for the coupling- logic.

Tapes manifest both in the operational semantics and in the logic.

Operationally, a tape consists of an upper bound
N\in\mathbb{N}
and a finite sequence of natural numbers less than or equal to
N
, representing future outcomes of
\operatorname{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{rand}}}(N)
commands.
Each tape is labeled with an identifier
\iota\in\kern-0.50003pt\textit{Label}
, and a program’s state is extended with a finite map from labels to tapes.
Tapes can be dynamically allocated using a
\operatorname{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{tape}}}
primitive:

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{tape}}}(N),\sigma

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{{}}^{1}\iota,\sigma[\iota\mapsto(N,\epsilon)]

\displaystyle\text{if}\penalty\ \iota=\fresh(\sigma)

which extends the mapping with an empty tape and the upper bound
N
, and it returns its fresh label
\iota
.
The
\operatorname{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{rand}}}
primitive can then optionally be annotated with a tape label
\iota
.
If
\sigma(\iota)=(N,\epsilon)
,
i.e.
, the corresponding tape is empty,
\operatorname{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{rand}}}(N,\iota)
reduces to any
n\leq N
with equal probability:

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{rand}}}(N,\iota),\sigma

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{{}}^{1/(N+1)}n,\sigma

\displaystyle\text{if }\sigma(\iota)=(N,\epsilon)\text{ and }n\leq N

but if the tape is
not
empty, the
\operatorname{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{rand}}}(N,\iota)
primitive reduces
deterministically
by taking off the first element of the tape and returning it:

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{rand}}}(N,\iota),\sigma

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{{}}^{1}n,\sigma[\iota\mapsto(N,\vec{n})\,]

\displaystyle\text{if}\penalty\ \sigma(\iota)=(N,n\cdot\vec{n})

If the tape bounds do not match, then
\operatorname{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{rand}}}(N,\iota)
reduces as if the tape was empty:

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{rand}}}(N,\iota),\sigma

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{{}}^{1/(N+1)}n,\sigma

\displaystyle\text{if}\penalty\ \sigma(\iota)=(M,\vec{n})\penalty\ \text{and}\penalty\ N\neq M\penalty\ \text{and}\penalty\ n\leq N

However,
no
primitives in the language add values to the tapes!
Instead, values are added to tapes as part of presampling steps that will be
ghost operations
appearing only in the relational logic.
That is, presampling will purely be a proof-device that has no operational effect: in the end, tapes can in fact be erased entirely through refinement as will be clear by the end of this section.

\vdots
K_{1}[e],
\iota\mapsto
x_{1}
x_{2}
\dots
x_{k}
K_{1}[e],
\iota\mapsto
x_{1}
x_{2}
\dots
x_{k}
\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{n}
K_{1}^{\prime}[\operatorname{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{rand}}}(\iota,N)],
\iota\mapsto
\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{n}
y_{1}
\dots
K_{1}^{\prime}[{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{n}}],
\iota\mapsto
y_{1}
\dots
(after
k
samples from
\iota
)
\vdots
K_{2}{[}\operatorname{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{rand}}}(N){]},\sigma^{\prime}
K{[}f({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{n}}){]},\sigma^{\prime}
\vdots

.

24
.

At the logical level, Clutch comes with a
\iota\hookrightarrow(N,\vec{n})
assertion that denotes
ownership
of the label
\iota
and its contents
(N,\vec{n})
, analogously to how the traditional points-to-connective
\ell\mapsto v
of separation logic denotes ownership of the location
\ell
and its contents on the heap.
When a tape is allocated, ownership of the fresh empty tape is acquired,
i.e.
,

\frac{\forall\iota.\penalty\thinspace\iota\hookrightarrow(N,\epsilon)\mskip 1.66667mu\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\hbox to10.29pt{\vbox to4.65pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25833pt\lower-2.32639pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}{{}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@roundcap\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 10.43 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{5.0347pt}{-2.32639pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{62}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}}}\mskip 1.66667mu\Delta\vDash_{\mathcal{E}}K{[}\iota{]}\precsim e:\tau}{\Delta\vDash_{\mathcal{E}}K{[}\operatorname{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{tape}}}(N){]}\precsim e:\tau}\quad\textsc{lab={rel-alloc-tape-l}}{}

Asynchronous couplings between probabilistic choices can be established in the refinement logic by coupling ghost presamplings with program steps.
For example, the rule below allows us to couple an (unlabeled) probabilistic choice on the right with a presampling on the
\iota
tape on the left:

\frac{f\penalty\ \text{bijection}\quad e\not\in\kern-1.99997pt\textit{Val}\quad\iota\hookrightarrow(N,\vec{n})\quad\forall n\leq N.\penalty\thinspace\iota\hookrightarrow(N,\vec{n}\cdot n)\mskip 1.66667mu\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\hbox to10.29pt{\vbox to4.65pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25833pt\lower-2.32639pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}{{}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@roundcap\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 10.43 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{5.0347pt}{-2.32639pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{62}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}}}\mskip 1.66667mu\Delta\vDash_{\mathcal{E}}e\precsim K^{\prime}{[}f(n){]}:\tau}{\Delta\vDash_{\mathcal{E}}e\precsim K^{\prime}{[}\operatorname{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{rand}}}(N){]}:\tau}\quad\textsc{lab={rel-couple-tape-l}}{}

Intuitively, as illustrated in
Figure

1
, the rule allows us to couple a logical
ghost
presampling step on the left (illustrated using a red dashed arrow) with a
physical
sampling on the right.
A symmetric rule holds for the opposite direction and two ghost presamplings can be coupled as well.
When we—at some point in the future—reach a presampled
\operatorname{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{rand}}}(N,\iota)
, we simply read off the presampled values from the
\iota
tape deterministically in a first-in-first-out order,
i.e.
,

\frac{\iota\hookrightarrow(N,n\cdot\vec{n})\quad\iota\hookrightarrow(N,\vec{n})\mskip 1.66667mu\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\hbox to10.29pt{\vbox to4.65pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25833pt\lower-2.32639pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}{{}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@roundcap\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 10.43 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{5.0347pt}{-2.32639pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{62}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}}}\mskip 1.66667mu\Delta\vDash_{\mathcal{E}}K{[}n{]}\precsim e_{2}:\tau}{\Delta\vDash_{\mathcal{E}}K{[}\operatorname{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{rand}}}(N,\iota){]}\precsim e_{2}:\tau}\quad\textsc{lab={rel-rand-tape-l}}{}

If we do not perform any presamplings, tapes and labels can be ignored and we can couple labeled sampling commands as if they were unlabeled:

\frac{\iota\hookrightarrow_{\mathsf{s}}(N,\epsilon)\quad\forall n\leq N.\penalty\thinspace\Delta\vDash_{\mathcal{E}}K{[}n{]}\precsim K^{\prime}{[}n{]}:\tau}{\Delta\vDash_{\mathcal{E}}K{[}\operatorname{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{rand}}}(N){]}\precsim K^{\prime}{[}\operatorname{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{rand}}}(N,\iota){]}:\tau}\quad\textsc{lab={rel-rand-erase-r}}{}

Here the assertion
\iota\hookrightarrow_{\mathsf{s}}(N,\epsilon)
denotes ownership of an empty tape
\iota
of the right-hand side program (the program on the “specification” side).

Example.

Using presampling tapes, we can show that
\mathit{lazy}
is a contextual refinement of
\mathit{eager}
from
Section

1
, that is,
\mathit{lazy}\precsim_{\text{ctx}}\mathit{eager}:\mathsf{unit}\to\mathsf{bool}
.
We first define an intermediate labeled version of
\mathit{lazy}
, using
\operatorname{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{flip}}}(\iota)\triangleq\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{if}}\penalty\thinspace\operatorname{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{rand}}}(1,\iota)=0\penalty\thinspace\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{then}}\penalty\thinspace\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{false}}\penalty\thinspace\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{else}}\penalty\thinspace\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{true}}
:

\displaystyle\mathit{lazy}^{\prime}\triangleq{}

\displaystyle\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{let}}\penalty\thinspace\iota\mathrel{=}\operatorname{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{tape}}}(1)\penalty\thinspace\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{in}}\penalty\thinspace

\displaystyle\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{let}}\penalty\thinspace r\mathrel{=}\operatorname{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{ref}}}(\operatorname{\mathsf{None}})\penalty\thinspace\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{in}}\penalty\thinspace

\displaystyle\lambda\_.\penalty\thinspace{\begin{array}[t]{rll}\lx@intercol\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{match}}\penalty\thinspace\mathop{!}r\penalty\thinspace\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{with}}\hfil\lx@intercol\\
&\operatorname{\mathsf{Some}}(b)&\Rightarrow b\\
|&\operatorname{\mathsf{None}}&\Rightarrow{\begin{array}[t]{l}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{let}}\penalty\thinspace b\mathrel{=}\operatorname{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{flip}}}(\iota)\penalty\thinspace\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{in}}\\
r\leftarrow\operatorname{\mathsf{Some}}(b);\\
b\end{array}}\\
\lx@intercol\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{end}}\hfil\lx@intercol\end{array}}

By transitivity of contextual refinement and
Theorem

1
it suffices to show
\vDash\mathit{lazy}\precsim\mathit{lazy}^{\prime}:\mathsf{unit}\to\mathsf{bool}
and
\vDash\mathit{lazy}^{\prime}\precsim\mathit{eager}:\mathsf{unit}\to\mathsf{bool}
.
The former follows straightforwardly using symbolic execution rules and
22
.
To show the latter we allocate a tape
\iota
and a reference
\ell
on the left by symbolic execution and couple the presampling of a
b\in\{0,1\}
on the
\iota
tape with the
\operatorname{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{flip}}}()
on the right using
24
. This establishes an
invariant

\displaystyle(\iota\hookrightarrow(1,b)\mskip 5.0mu{\ast}\mskip 5.0mu\ell\mapsto\operatorname{\mathsf{None}})\lor\ell\mapsto\operatorname{\mathsf{Some}}(b)

that expresses how either
b
is on the
\iota
tape and the location
\ell
is empty
or

\ell
contains the value
b
.
Invariants are particular kinds of propositions in Clutch that, in this particular case, are guaranteed to always hold at the beginning and at the end of the function evaluation.
Under this invariant, we show that the two thunks are related by symbolic execution and rules for accessing invariants that we detail in
Section

4
.
Symmetric arguments allow us to show the refinement in the other direction and consequently the contextual equivalence.

This example shows how presampling tapes are simple and powerful, yet merely a proof-device: the final equivalence holds for programs without any mention of tapes.
Intuitively, tapes allow us to separate the process of building a coupling from the operational semantics of the program.
One might be tempted to believe, though, that as soon as the idea of presampling arises, the high-level proof rules as supported by Clutch are straightforward to state and prove.
This is
not
the case. As we will show throughout the paper, a great deal of care goes into defining a system that supports presampling while being sound.
In
Section

7
we discuss two counterexamples that illustrate some of the subtleties involved in defining a sound system.

3.
Preliminaries and the Language
\mathbf{F}_{\mu,\textnormal{ref}}^{\textnormal{rand}}

To account for non-terminating behavior, we will define our operational semantics using probability sub-distributions which we recall below.

Definition 1 (Sub-distribution).

A (discrete)
sub-distribution
over a countable set
A
is a function
\mu:A\to[0,1]
such that
\sum_{a\in A}\mu(a)\leq 1
.
We write
\mathcal{D}(A)
for the set of all sub-distributions over
A
.

Definition 2 (Support).

The
support
of
\mu\in\mathcal{D}(A)
is the set of elements

\supp(\mu)\triangleq{}\left\{a\in A\mid\mu(a)>0\right\}

Lemma 3 (Probability Monad).

Let
\mu\in\mathcal{D}(A)
,
a\in A
, and
f:A\to\mathcal{D}(B)
.
Then

(1)

\mbind(f,\mu)(b)\triangleq{}\sum_{a\in A}\mu(a)\cdot f(a)(b)

(2)

\mret(a)(a^{\prime})\triangleq{}\begin{cases}1&\text{if }a=a^{\prime}\\
0&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

gives monadic structure to
\mathcal{D}
.
We write
\mu\mathbin{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\gg\mkern-6.0mu=$}}}f
for
\mbind(f,\mu)
.

The syntax of the language
\mathbf{F}_{\mu,\textnormal{ref}}^{\textnormal{rand}}{}
is defined by the grammar below.

\displaystyle v,w\in\kern-1.99997pt\textit{Val}\vcentcolon\vcentcolon={}

\displaystyle z\in\mathbb{Z}\ |\ b\in\mathbb{B}\ |\ ()\ |\ \ell\in\kern-0.50003pt\textit{Loc}\ |\ \iota\in\kern-0.50003pt\textit{Label}\ |\ \mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{rec}}\penalty\thinspace\mathit{f}\penalty\thinspace\mathit{x}\mathrel{=}e\ |\ (v,w)\ |\ \operatorname{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{inl}}}(v)\ |\ \operatorname{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{inr}}}(v)

\displaystyle e\in\kern-0.50003pt\textit{Expr}\vcentcolon\vcentcolon={}

\displaystyle v\ |\ \mathit{x}\ |\ e_{1}(e_{2})\ |\ \mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{if}}\penalty\thinspace e\penalty\thinspace\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{then}}\penalty\thinspace e_{1}\penalty\thinspace\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{else}}\penalty\thinspace e_{2}\ |\ \operatorname{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{fst}}}(e)\ |\ \operatorname{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{snd}}}(e)\ |\ \operatorname{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{ref}}}(e)\ |\ \mathop{!}e\ |\ e_{1}\leftarrow e_{2}\ |\

\displaystyle\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{match}}\penalty\thinspace e\penalty\thinspace\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{with}}\penalty\thinspace\operatorname{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{inl}}}(v)\Rightarrow e_{1}\mid\operatorname{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{inr}}}(w)\Rightarrow e_{2}\penalty\thinspace\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{end}}\ |\ \mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{fold}}\penalty\thinspace e\ |\ \mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{unfold}}\penalty\thinspace e\ |\ \Lambda e\ |\ e\penalty\ \_\ |\

\displaystyle\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{pack}}\penalty\thinspace e\ |\ \mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{unpack}}\penalty\thinspace e\penalty\thinspace\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{as}}\penalty\thinspace x\penalty\thinspace\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{in}}\penalty\thinspace e\ |\ \operatorname{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{tape}}}(e)\ |\ \operatorname{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{rand}}}(e_{1},e_{2})\ |\ e_{1}+e_{2}\ |\ e_{1}-e_{2}\ |\ \cdots

\displaystyle K\in\kern-0.50003pt\textit{Ectx}\vcentcolon\vcentcolon={}

\displaystyle-\ |\ e\,K\ |\ K\,v\ |\ \mathop{!}K\ |\ e\leftarrow K\ |\ K\leftarrow v\ |\ \operatorname{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{tape}}}(K)\ |\ \operatorname{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{rand}}}(e,K)\ |\ \operatorname{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{rand}}}(K,v)\ |\ \ldots

\displaystyle\sigma\in\kern-0.50003pt\textit{State}\triangleq{}

\displaystyle(\kern-0.50003pt\textit{Loc}\xrightharpoonup{\smash{\raisebox{-0.90417pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.52742pt\mathsf{fin}\kern-0.21098pt$}}}\kern-1.99997pt\textit{Val})\times(\kern-0.50003pt\textit{Label}\xrightharpoonup{\smash{\raisebox{-0.90417pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.52742pt\mathsf{fin}\kern-0.21098pt$}}}\kern-0.50003pt\textit{Tape})

\displaystyle t\in\kern-0.50003pt\textit{Tape}\triangleq{}

\displaystyle\{(N,\vec{n})\mid N\in\mathbb{N}\wedge\vec{n}\in\mathbb{N}_{\leq N}^{\ast}\}

\displaystyle\rho\in\kern-0.50003pt\textit{Cfg}\triangleq{}

\displaystyle\kern-0.50003pt\textit{Expr}\times\kern-0.50003pt\textit{State}

\displaystyle\tau\in\kern-0.50003pt\textit{Type}\vcentcolon\vcentcolon={}

\displaystyle\alpha\ |\ \mathsf{unit}\ |\ \mathsf{bool}\ |\ \mathsf{nat}\ |\ \mathsf{int}\ |\ \tau\times\tau\ |\ \tau+\tau\ |\ \tau\to\tau\ |\ \forall\alpha.\penalty\thinspace\tau\ |\ \exists\alpha.\penalty\thinspace\tau\ |\ \mu\,\alpha.\penalty\thinspace\tau\ |\ \mathsf{ref}\penalty\thinspace\tau\ |\ \mathsf{tape}

The term language is mostly standard but note that there are no types in terms; we write
\Lambda e
for type abstraction and
e\penalty\ \_
for type application.

\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{fold}}\penalty\thinspace e
and
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{unfold}}\penalty\thinspace e
are the special term constructs for iso-recursive types.

\operatorname{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{ref}}}(e)
allocates a new reference,
\mathop{!}e
dereferences the location
e
evaluates to, and
e_{1}\leftarrow e_{2}
assigns the result of evaluating
e_{2}
to the location that
e_{1}
evaluates to.
We introduce syntactic sugar for lambda abstractions
\lambda x.\penalty\thinspace e
defined as
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{rec}}\penalty\thinspace\_\penalty\thinspace x\mathrel{=}e
,
let-bindings
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{let}}\penalty\thinspace x\mathrel{=}e_{1}\penalty\thinspace\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{in}}\penalty\thinspace e_{2}
defined as
(\lambda x.\penalty\thinspace e_{2})(e_{1})
, and sequencing
e_{1};e_{2}
defined as
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{let}}\penalty\thinspace\_\mathrel{=}e_{1}\penalty\thinspace\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{in}}\penalty\thinspace e_{2}
.
We write
\operatorname{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{rand}}}(N)
for
\operatorname{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{rand}}}(N,())
,
i.e.
an unlabeled probabilistic choice.

We implicitly coerce from
\sigma\in\kern-0.50003pt\textit{State}
to heaps and tapes,
e.g.
,
\sigma(\ell)=\pi_{1}(\sigma)(\ell)
and
\sigma(\iota)=\pi_{2}(\sigma)(\iota)
.
Tapes are formally pairs
(N,\vec{n})
of
N\in\mathbb{N}
and a finite sequence
\vec{n}
of natural numbers less than or equal to
N
.
The language has a call-by-value single-step-reduction relation
\xrightarrow{{}}\subseteq\kern-0.50003pt\textit{Cfg}\times[0,1]\times\kern-0.50003pt\textit{Cfg}
defined using evaluation contexts
K\in\kern-0.50003pt\textit{Ectx}
.
The relation is mostly standard: all the non-probabilistic constructs reduce as usual with weight
1
and
\operatorname{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{rand}}}(e_{1},e_{2})
reduces as discussed in
Section

2
.

To define full program execution, let
\stepdistr(\rho)\in\mathcal{D}(\kern-0.50003pt\textit{Cfg})
denote the distribution induced by the single step reduction of configuration
\rho\in\kern-0.50003pt\textit{Cfg}
.
First, we define a stratified execution probability
\execVal_{n}\colon\kern-0.50003pt\textit{Cfg}\to\mathcal{D}(\kern-1.99997pt\textit{Val})
by induction on
n
:

\displaystyle\execVal_{n}(e,\sigma)\triangleq{}\begin{cases}\mathbf{0}&\text{if}\penalty\ e\not\in\kern-1.99997pt\textit{Val}\penalty\ \text{and}\penalty\ n=0\\
\mret(e)&\text{if}\penalty\ e\in\kern-1.99997pt\textit{Val}\\
\stepdistr(e,\sigma)\mathbin{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\gg\mkern-6.0mu=$}}}\execVal_{(n-1)}&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

where
\mathbf{0}
denotes the everywhere-zero distribution.
That is,
\execVal_{n}(e,\sigma)(v)
denotes the probability of stepping from the configuration
(e,\sigma)
to a value
v
in less than
n
steps.
The probability that a full execution, starting from configuration
\rho
, reaches a value
v
is the limit of its stratified approximations, which exists by monotonicity and boundedness:

\displaystyle\execVal(\rho)(v)\triangleq{}\mathsf{lim}_{n\to\infty}\execVal_{n}(\rho)(v)

The probability that a full execution from a starting configuration
\rho
terminates then becomes
\execVal_{\Downarrow}(\rho)\triangleq{}\sum_{v\in\kern-1.63885pt\textit{Val}}\execVal(\rho)(v)
.

Typing judgments have the form
\Theta\mid\Gamma\vdash e:\tau
where
\Gamma
is a context assigning types to program variables, and
\Theta
is a context of type variables that may occur in
\Gamma
and
\tau
.
The inference rules for the typing judgments are standard (see,
e.g.
,
Frumin et al. 2021b
or the Coq formalization) and omitted, except for the straightforward rules for typing tapes and samplings shown below:

\frac{\Theta\mid\Gamma\vdash e:\mathsf{nat}}{\Theta\mid\Gamma\vdash\operatorname{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{tape}}}(e):\mathsf{tape}}\quad\textsc{lab={t-tape}}\frac{\Theta\mid\Gamma\vdash e_{1}:\mathsf{nat}\quad\Theta\mid\Gamma\vdash e_{2}:\tau\quad\tau=\mathsf{unit}\lor\tau=\mathsf{tape}}{\Theta\mid\Gamma\vdash\operatorname{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{rand}}}(e_{1},e_{2}):\mathsf{nat}}\quad\textsc{lab={t-rand}}

The notion of contextual refinement that we use is also mostly standard and uses the termination probability
\execVal_{\Downarrow}
as observation predicate.
Since we are in a typed setting, we consider only typed contexts.
A program context is well-typed, written
\mathcal{C}:(\Theta\mid\Gamma\vdash\tau)\Rightarrow(\Theta^{\prime}\mid\Gamma^{\prime}\vdash\tau^{\prime})
, if for any term
e
such that
\Theta\mid\Gamma\vdash e:\tau
we have
\Theta^{\prime}\mid\Gamma^{\prime}\vdash\mathcal{C}{[}e{]}:\tau^{\prime}
.
We say expression
e_{1}

contextually refines
expression
e_{2}
if for all well-typed program contexts
\mathcal{C}
resulting in a closed program then the termination probability of
\mathcal{C}{[}e_{1}{]}
is bounded by the termination probability of
\mathcal{C}{[}e_{2}{]}
:

\displaystyle\Theta\mid\Gamma\vdash e_{1}\precsim_{\text{ctx}}e_{2}:\tau\triangleq{}

\displaystyle\forall\tau^{\prime},(\mathcal{C}:(\Theta\mid\Gamma\vdash\tau)\Rightarrow(\emptyset\mid\emptyset\vdash\tau^{\prime})),\sigma.\penalty\thinspace

\displaystyle\quad\execVal_{\Downarrow}(\mathcal{C}{[}e_{1}{]},\sigma)\leq\execVal_{\Downarrow}(\mathcal{C}{[}e_{2}{]},\sigma)

Note that contextual refinement is a precongruence, and that the statement itself is in the meta-logic (
e.g.
, Coq) and makes no mention of Clutch or Iris.
Contextual equivalence
\Theta\mid\Gamma\vdash e_{1}\simeq_{\text{ctx}}e_{2}:\tau
is defined as the symmetric interior of refinement:
(\Theta\mid\Gamma\vdash e_{1}\precsim_{\text{ctx}}e_{2}:\tau)\land(\Theta\mid\Gamma\vdash e_{2}\precsim_{\text{ctx}}e_{1}:\tau)
.

4.
The Clutch Refinement Logic

In the style of ReLoC
(
Frumin et al. 2021b
)
, we define a
logical refinement judgment

\Delta\vDash_{\mathcal{E}}e_{1}\precsim e_{2}:\tau
as an internal notion in the Clutch separation logic by structural recursion over the type
\tau
.
The fundamental theorem of logical relations will then show that logical refinement implies contextual refinement.
This means proving contextual refinement can be reduced to proving logical refinement, which is generally much easier.
When defining and proving logical refinement, we can leverage the features of modern separation logic,
e.g.
, (impredicative) invariants and (higher-order) ghost state as inherited from Iris, to model and reason about complex programs and language features.

Clutch is based on higher-order intuitionistic separation logic and the most important propositions are shown below.

\displaystyle P,Q\in\textit{iProp}\vcentcolon\vcentcolon={}

\displaystyle\mathsf{True}\ |\ \mathsf{False}\ |\ P\land Q\ |\ P\lor Q\ |\ P\Rightarrow Q\ |\ \forall x.\penalty\thinspace P\ |\ \exists x.\penalty\thinspace P\ |\ P\mskip 5.0mu{\ast}\mskip 5.0muQ\ |\ P\mskip 1.66667mu\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\hbox to10.29pt{\vbox to4.65pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25833pt\lower-2.32639pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}{{}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@roundcap\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 10.43 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{5.0347pt}{-2.32639pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{62}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}}}\mskip 1.66667muQ\ |\

, which we will introduce as needed.
The proposition
\ulcorner\phi\urcorner
embeds a meta-logic (
e.g.
, Coq) proposition
\phi
(
e.g.
, equality or a coupling) into Clutch but we will omit the brackets whenever the type of
\phi
is clear from the context.

Like ordinary separation logic, Clutch has heap points-to assertions. Since the logic is relational, these come in two forms:
\ell\mapsto v
for the left-hand side program’s state and
\ell\mapsto_{\mathsf{s}}v
for the right-hand side’s state (the “specification” side).
For the same reason, tape assertions come in two forms as well,
\iota\hookrightarrow(N,\vec{n})
and
\iota\hookrightarrow_{\mathsf{s}}(N,\vec{n})
respectively.

4.1.
Refinement judgments

The refinement judgment
\Delta\vDash_{\mathcal{E}}e_{1}\precsim e_{2}:\tau
should be read as “in environment
\Delta
, the expression
e_{1}
refines the expression
e_{2}
at type
\tau
under the invariants in
\mathcal{E}
”.
We refer to
e_{1}
as the
implementation
and to
e_{2}
as the
specification
.
The environment
\Delta
assigns
interpretations
to type variables occurring in
\tau
.
These interpretations are Clutch relations of type
\kern-1.99997pt\textit{Val}\times\kern-1.99997pt\textit{Val}\to\textit{iProp}
.
One such relation is the binary interpretation
\llbracket\tau\rrbracket_{\Delta}(-,-)
of a syntactic type
\tau\in\kern-0.50003pt\textit{Type}
which is used to define the refinement judgment, as discussed in
Section

5.2
.
For example, for base types such as
\mathsf{bool}
and
\mathsf{int}
, the value interpretation asserts equality between the values.

Figure

21
showcases a selection of the type-directed structural and computational rules for proving logical refinement for deterministic reductions.
Our computational rules resemble the typical forward-symbolic-execution-style rules from,
e.g.
, the weakest precondition calculus in Iris
(
Jung et al. 2018
)
, but come in forms for both the left-hand side and the right-hand side.
For example,
21
and
21
symbolically execute “pure” reductions,
i.e.
reductions that do not depend on the state, such as
\beta
-reductions.

21
and
21
on the other hand depend on the heap and require ownership of a location to store values at it.
We remark that all the rules for the deterministic fragment of the Clutch refinement judgment are identical to the rules for the sequential fragment of the non-probabilistic relational logic ReLoC
(
Frumin et al. 2021b
)
—even though the underlying semantics and model are very different.
This is one of the key reasons behind the support for modular reasoning.

Figure 3
.
Rules for non-relational probabilistic choices and tapes for the Clutch refinement judgment.

The main novelty of Clutch is the support for both synchronous and asynchronous couplings for which rules are shown in
Figure

24
.

24
is a classical coupling rule that relates two samplings that can be aligned, just like
1
as we saw in
Section

1
.
The rules
24
and
24
, on the other hand, are asynchronous coupling rules; they both couple a sampling reduction with an arbitrary expression on the opposite side by presampling a coupled value to a tape, as discussed in
Section

2
.
Finally,
24
couples two ghost presamplings to two tapes, and hence offers full asynchrony.

4.2.
Persistence and invariants

As mentioned above, the environment
\Delta
in Clutch’s refinement judgement provides an interpretation of types as relations in the logic.
However, Clutch is a substructural separation logic, while the type system of
\mathbf{F}_{\mu,\textnormal{ref}}^{\textnormal{rand}}
is
not
substructural.
To account for the non-substructural nature of
\mathbf{F}_{\mu,\textnormal{ref}}^{\textnormal{rand}}
’s types, we make use of the
persistence modality

\mathop{\boxempty}
.
We say
P
is persistent, written
\mathsf{persistent}(P)
if
P\vdash\mathop{\boxempty}P
; otherwise, we say that
P
is
ephemeral
.
Persistent resources can freely be duplicated (
\mathop{\boxempty}P\mathrel{\dashv\vdash}\mathop{\boxempty}P\mskip 5.0mu{\ast}\mskip 5.0mu\mathop{\boxempty}P
) and eliminated (
\mathop{\boxempty}P\vdash P
).
For example, invariants and non-atomic invariants are persistent: once established, they will remain true forever.
On the contrary, ephemeral propositions like the points-to connective
\ell\mapsto v
for the heap may be invalidated in the future when the location is updated.
For exactly this reason, the rule
21
also requires the interpretation of the type variable to be persistent, to guarantee that it does not depend on ephemeral resources.

To reason about,
e.g.
, functions that make use of ephemeral resources, a common pattern is to “put them in an invariant” to make them persistent, as sketched in
Section

2
for the lazy/eager example.
Since our language is sequential, when a function is invoked, no other code can execute before the function returns.
This means that we can soundly keep invariants “open” and temporarily invalidate them for the entire duration of a function invocation—as long as the invariants are reestablished before returning.
Non-atomic invariants allow us to capture exactly this intuition.

Invariants are annotated with invariant names
\mathcal{N}\in\textit{InvName}
and the refinement judgment is annotated by
invariant masks

\mathcal{E}\subseteq\textit{InvName}
that indicates which non-atomic invariants that are currently
closed
.
This is needed for bookkeeping of the invariant mechanism in order to avoid reentrancy issues, where invariants are opened in a nested (and unsound) fashion.

Figure

25
shows structural rules for the refinement judgment’s interaction with non-atomic invariants.
An invariant
\hbox to13.34pt{\vbox to9.43pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 6.67113pt\lower-0.8pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {

can be allocated (
25
) by giving up ownership of
P
.
When opening an invariant (
25
) one obtains the resources
P
together with a resource
\mathsf{closeNaInv}_{\mathcal{N}}(P)
that allows one to close the invariant again (
25
) by reestablishing
P
.
We guarantee that all invariants are closed by the end of evaluation by requiring
\top
, the set of all invariant names, as mask annotation on the judgment in all value cases (see,
e.g.
,
21
,
21
, and
21
in
Figure

21
).

Clutch invariants are inherited from Iris and hence they are
impredicative

(
Svendsen and Birkedal 2014
)
which means that the proposition
P
in

and can,

To ensure soundness of the logic and avoid self-referential paradoxes, invariant access guards
P
by the later modality
\mathop{{\triangleright}}
.
When invariants are not used impredicatively, the later modality can mostly be ignored as we have done and will do throughout the paper.
The later modality is essential for the soundness of the logical relation and taking guarded fixpoints
\mu x.\penalty\thinspace P
that require the recursive occurrence
x
to appear under the later modality, but our use is entirely standard.
We refer to
Jung et al. 2018
for more details on the later modality and how it is generally used in Iris.

5.
Model of Clutch

In this section we show how the connectives of Clutch are modeled through a shallow embedding in the
base logic
of the Iris separation logic
(
Jung et al. 2018
)
.
First, we describe how we define a relational
coupling logic
(
Section

5.1
) that is used to establish couplings between programs.
Next, we show how the coupling logic in combination with a binary interpretation of types is used to define the refinement logic (
Section

5.2
).
Finally, we summarize how the final soundness theorem is proven (
Section

5.3
).

The general structure and skeleton of our model mimics the construction of several
non
-probabilistic logical relations found in prior work
(
Turon et al. 2013b
;
Turon et al. 2013a
;
Krebbers et al. 2017b
;
Frumin et al. 2021b
)
.
A key contribution and benefit of Clutch is that that same structure can be adapted to handle
probabilistic
refinements through the right choice of intermediate definitions and abstractions, as we will highlight throughout this section.
While some aspects of the model are Iris-specific, the key ideas are general and should apply to other frameworks as well.

5.1.
Coupling logic

We recall that probabilistic couplings are used to prove relations between distributions by constructing a joint distribution that relates two distributions in a particularly desirable way:

Definition 1 (Coupling).

Let
\mu_{1}\in\mathcal{D}(A)
,
\mu_{2}\in\mathcal{D}(B)
. A sub-distribution
\mu\in\mathcal{D}(A\times B)
is a
coupling
of
\mu_{1}
and
\mu_{2}
if

(1)

\forall a.\penalty\thinspace\sum_{b\in B}\mu(a,b)=\mu_{1}(a)

(2)

\forall b.\penalty\thinspace\sum_{a\in A}\mu(a,b)=\mu_{2}(b)

Given a relation
R\subseteq A\times B
we say
\mu
is an
R
-coupling if furthermore
\supp(\mu)\subseteq R
.
We write
\mu_{1}\sim\mu_{2}:R
if there exists an
R
-coupling of
\mu_{1}
and
\mu_{2}
.

Couplings can be constructed and composed along the monadic structure of sub-distributions.

Lemma 2 (Composition of couplings).

Let
R\subseteq A\times B
,
S\subseteq A^{\prime}\times B^{\prime}
,
\mu_{1}\in\mathcal{D}(A)
,
\mu_{2}\in\mathcal{D}(B)
,
f_{1}:A\to\mathcal{D}(A^{\prime})
, and
f_{2}:B\to\mathcal{D}(B^{\prime})
.

(1)

If
(a,b)\in R
then
\mret(a)\sim\mret(b):R
.

(2)

If
\mu_{1}\sim\mu_{2}:R
and for all
(a,b)\in R
it is the case that
f_{1}(a)\sim f_{2}(b):S
then
\mu_{1}\mathbin{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\gg\mkern-6.0mu=$}}}f_{1}\sim\mu_{2}\mathbin{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\gg\mkern-6.0mu=$}}}f_{2}:S

Once a coupling has been established, we can often extract a concrete relation from it between the probability distributions.
In particular, for
(=)
-couplings, we have the following result.

Lemma 3.

If
\mu_{1}\sim\mu_{2}:(=)
then
\mu_{1}=\mu_{2}
.

The Clutch coupling logic can be seen as a higher-order separation logic analogue of
Barthe et al. 2015
’s pRHL logic.
However, unlike pRHL, which uses the four-part Hoare
quadruples
that we saw in
Section

1
to do relational reasoning, the coupling logic instead follows CaReSL
(
Turon et al. 2013a
)
and encodes one of the programs as a separation logic
ghost resource
.
In particular, the coupling logic consists of two components: (1) a
unary
weakest precondition theory
\mathsf{wp}\penalty\thinspace e\penalty\thinspace{\left\{\Phi\right\}}
; and (2) a
specification resource

\mathsf{spec}(e^{\prime})
with
specification context

\mathsf{specCtx}
.
We think of the program
e
in the weakest precondition predicate as representing the program that occurs on the left side of a quadruple, while the specification program
e^{\prime}
represents the right side program.
The specification context assertion
\mathsf{specCtx}
will be used to connect the weakest precondition to the specification resource.
Ultimately, by showing

\displaystyle\mathsf{specCtx}\mskip 5.0mu{\ast}\mskip 5.0mu\mathsf{spec}(e^{\prime})\vdash\mathsf{wp}\penalty\thinspace e\penalty\thinspace{\left\{v.\exists v^{\prime}.\penalty\thinspace\mathsf{spec}(v^{\prime})\mskip 5.0mu{\ast}\mskip 5.0mu\varphi(v,v^{\prime})\right\}}

in the logic, we will have established a
\varphi
-coupling of the executions of the programs
e
and
e^{\prime}
.

The weakest precondition.

The weakest precondition connective
\mathsf{wp}\penalty\thinspace e\penalty\thinspace{\left\{v.\Phi\right\}}
is a new probabilistic weakest precondition that we formally define below.
In isolation it simply means that the execution of
e
is safe (
i.e.
, the probability of crashing is zero), and for every possible return value
v
of
e
, the postcondition
\Phi(v)
holds.
Note however, that it encodes
partial
correctness, as it does not imply that the probability of termination is necessarily one, meaning the program may diverge.

In most Iris-style program logics, the weakest precondition
\mathsf{wp}\penalty\thinspace e\penalty\thinspace{\left\{\Phi\right\}}
is a predicate stating that either the program
e
is a value satisfying
\Phi
or it is reducible such that for any other term
e^{\prime}
that it reduces to, then
\mathsf{wp}\penalty\thinspace e^{\prime}\penalty\thinspace{\left\{\Phi\right\}}
must hold as well.
This guarantees safety of the full execution of the program
e
.
The weakest precondition that we define in this section has—in isolation—the same intuition but it is fundamentally different.
It is still a
unary
predicate, but in order to do relational reasoning, the weakest precondition pairs up the probability distribution of individual program steps of the left-hand side with the probability distribution of individual steps of
some
other program in such a way that there exists a
probabilistic coupling
among them.
Through the
\mathsf{specCtx}
we will guarantee that this “other” program is tied to the program tracked by the
\mathsf{spec}(e^{\prime})
resource.
The weakest precondition itself satisfies all the usual structural rules such as
20
and
20
found in
Figure

20
as well as language-level primitive rules such as
20
, but in combination with the
\mathsf{specCtx}
and
\mathsf{spec}(e^{\prime})
resources, the coupling logic satisfies rules like
20
and
20
. Notice the resemblance between
20
and
1
from
Section

1
.

The weakest precondition connective is given by a guarded fixpoint of the equation below---the fixpoint exists because the recursive occurrence appears under the later modality.

We omit from the definition occurrences of the Iris
fancy update modality
needed for resource updates and necessary book-keeping related to Iris invariants—these matters are essential but our use is entirely standard. For the Iris expert we refer to

for the full definition.

\displaystyle\mathsf{wp}\penalty\thinspace e_{1}\penalty\thinspace{\left\{\Phi\right\}}\triangleq{}

\displaystyle(e_{1}\in\kern-1.99997pt\textit{Val}\mathrel{\land}\Phi(e_{1}))\lor{}

\displaystyle(e_{1}\not\in\kern-1.99997pt\textit{Val}\mathrel{\land}\forall\sigma_{1},\rho^{\prime}_{1}.\penalty\thinspace S(\sigma_{1})\mskip 5.0mu{\ast}\mskip 5.0muG(\rho^{\prime}_{1})\mskip 1.66667mu\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\hbox to10.29pt{\vbox to4.65pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25833pt\lower-2.32639pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}

\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}}}\mskip 1.66667mu

\displaystyle\quad\mathsf{execCoupl}((e_{1},\sigma_{1}),\rho^{\prime}_{1})(\lambda(e_{2},\sigma_{2}),\rho^{\prime}_{2}.\penalty\thinspace\mathop{{\triangleright}}S(\sigma_{2})\mskip 5.0mu{\ast}\mskip 5.0muG(\rho^{\prime}_{2})\mskip 5.0mu{\ast}\mskip 5.0mu\mathsf{wp}_{\mathcal{E}}\penalty\thinspace e_{2}\penalty\thinspace{\left\{\Phi\right\}}))

The base case says that if the expression
e_{1}
is a value then the postcondition
\Phi(e_{1})
must hold.
On the other hand, if
e_{1}
is
not
a value, we get to assume two propositions
S(\sigma_{1})
and
G(\rho^{\prime}_{1})
for any
\sigma_{1}\in\kern-0.50003pt\textit{State},{\rho_{1}}^{\prime}\in\kern-0.50003pt\textit{Cfg}
, and then we must prove
\mathsf{execCoupl}((e_{1},\sigma_{1}),\rho^{\prime}_{1})(\ldots)
.
The
S:\kern-0.50003pt\textit{State}\to\textit{iProp}
predicate is a
state interpretation
that interprets the state (the heap and the tapes) of the language as resources in Clutch and gives meaning to the
\ell\mapsto v
and
\iota\hookrightarrow(N,\vec{n})
connectives.
The
G:\kern-0.50003pt\textit{Cfg}\to\textit{iProp}
predicate is a
specification interpretation
that allows us to interpret and track the “other” program that we are constructing a coupling with—we return to its instantiation momentarily.

The key technical novelty and the essence of the weakest precondition is the
coupling modality
: Intuitively, the proposition
\mathsf{execCoupl}(\rho_{1},\rho^{\prime}_{1})(\lambda\rho_{2},\rho^{\prime}_{2}.\penalty\thinspace P)
says that there exists a series of (composable) couplings starting from configurations
\rho_{1}
and
\rho^{\prime}_{1}
that ends up in some configurations
\rho_{2}
and
\rho^{\prime}_{2}
such that the proposition
P
holds.
With this intuition in mind, the last clause of the weakest precondition says that the execution of
(e_{1},\sigma_{1})
can be coupled with the execution of
\rho^{\prime}_{1}
such that the state and specification interpretations still hold for the end configurations, and the weakest precondition holds recursively for the continuation
e_{2}
.

Coupling modality

The coupling modality is an inductively defined proposition in Clutch, formally defined as a least fixpoint of an equation with six different disjuncts found in
Appendix

A
.
The modality supports both synchronous and asynchronous couplings on both sides while ensuring that the left program takes at least one step.
As it is inductively defined, we can chain together multiple couplings but it always ends in base cases that couple a single step of the left-hand side program—this aligns with the usual intuition that each unfolding of the recursively defined weakest precondition corresponds to one physical program step.

For instance, we can couple two physical program steps through the following constructor:

\frac{\mathsf{red}(\rho_{1})\quad\stepdistr(\rho_{1})\sim\stepdistr(\rho_{1}^{\prime}):R\quad\forall\rho_{2},{\rho_{2}}^{\prime}.\penalty\thinspace R(\rho_{2},{\rho_{2}}^{\prime})\mskip 1.66667mu\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\hbox to10.29pt{\vbox to4.65pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25833pt\lower-2.32639pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}{{}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@roundcap\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 10.43 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{5.0347pt}{-2.32639pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{62}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}}}\mskip 1.66667muZ(\rho_{2},{\rho_{2}}^{\prime})}{\mathsf{execCoupl}(\rho_{1},\rho_{1}^{\prime})(Z)}

Intuitively, this says that to show
\mathsf{execCoupl}(\rho_{1},{\rho_{1}}^{\prime})(Z)
we (1) have to show that the configuration
\rho_{1}
is
red
ucible which means that the program
can
take a step (this is to guarantee safety of the left-hand side program), (2) pick a relation
R
and show that there exists an
R
-coupling of the two program steps, and (3) for all configurations
\rho_{2},{\rho_{2}}^{\prime}
in the support of the coupling, the logical predicate
Z(\rho_{2},{\rho_{2}}^{\prime})
holds.
This rule is used to justify the classical coupling rule
20
that (synchronously) couples two program samplings.

The coupling modality also allows to construct a coupling between a program step and a trivial (Dirac) distribution; this is used to validate proof rules that symbolically execute just one of the two sides.
Indeed, the rule below allows us to progress the right-hand side independently from the left-hand side, but notice the occurrence of the coupling modality in the premise—this allows us to chain multiple couplings together in a single coupling modality.

\frac{\mret(\rho_{1})\sim\stepdistr(\rho^{\prime}_{1}):R\quad\forall{\rho_{2}}^{\prime}.\penalty\thinspace R(\rho_{1},{\rho_{2}}^{\prime})\mskip 1.66667mu\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\hbox to10.29pt{\vbox to4.65pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25833pt\lower-2.32639pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}

To support
asynchronous
couplings, we introduce a
state step
reduction relation
\xrightarrow{{}}_{\iota}\subseteq\kern-0.50003pt\textit{State}\times[0,1]\times\kern-0.50003pt\textit{State}
that uniformly at random samples a natural number
n
to the end of the tape
\iota
:

\displaystyle\sigma\xrightarrow{{}}_{\iota}^{1/(N+1)}\sigma[\iota\to(N,\vec{n}\cdot n)]

\displaystyle\text{if }\sigma(\iota)=(N,\vec{n})\text{ and }n\leq N

Let
\statestepdistr_{\iota}(\sigma)
denote the induced distribution of a single state step reduction of
\sigma
.
The coupling modality allows us to introduce couplings between
\statestepdistr_{\iota}(\sigma)
and a sampling step:

\frac{\statestepdistr_{\iota}(\sigma_{1})\sim\stepdistr({\rho_{1}}^{\prime}):R\quad\forall\sigma_{2},{\rho_{2}}^{\prime}.\penalty\thinspace R(\sigma_{2},{\rho_{2}}^{\prime})\mskip 1.66667mu\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\hbox to10.29pt{\vbox to4.65pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25833pt\lower-2.32639pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}

\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}}}\mskip 1.66667mu\mathsf{execCoupl}((e_{1},\sigma_{2}),{\rho_{2}}^{\prime})(Z)}{\mathsf{execCoupl}((e_{1},\sigma_{1}),{\rho_{1}}^{\prime})(Z)}

Note that here the left-hand side program does not take a physical step, thus the coupling modality appears in the premise as well.
This particular rule is key to the soundness of the asynchronous coupling rule
20
that couples a sampling to a tape on the left with a program sampling on the right.
We use similar constructors of
\mathsf{execCoupl}
to prove,
e.g.

24
.
The crux is, however, that the extra state steps that we inject in the coupling modality to prove the asynchronous coupling rules
do not matter
(!)
in the sense that they can be entirely erased as part of the coupling logic’s adequacy theorem (
Theorem

7
).

A specification resource and context with run ahead

We will encode a relational specification into a unary specification by proving a unary weakest precondition about
e
(the
implementation
), in which
e^{\prime}
(the
specification
) is tracked using a ghost resource
\mathsf{spec}(e^{\prime})
that can be updated to reflect execution steps.
The ghost specification connective
\mathsf{spec}(e^{\prime})
, together with the
\mathsf{specCtx}
proposition, satisfies a number of symbolic execution rules following the operational semantics.

The
\mathsf{specCtx}
proposition is an Iris invariant and its purpose is twofold: (1) it gives meaning to the ghost specification resource
\mathsf{spec}(e)
and the heap and tape assertions,
\ell\mapsto_{\mathsf{s}}v
and
\iota\hookrightarrow_{\mathsf{s}}(N,\vec{n})
, and (2) it connects the
\mathsf{spec}(e)
resource to the program
e^{\prime}
that we are constructing a coupling with in the weakest precondition.
We keep track of
e^{\prime}
through the specification interpretation
G
.
When constructing a final closed proof we will want
e
to be equal to
e^{\prime}
, however, during proofs they are not always going to be the same—we will allow
e
to
run ahead
of
e^{\prime}
.
As a consequence, it will be possible to reason
independently
about the right-hand side without consideration of the left-hand side as exemplified by the rules below
4
4

4

Technically, the consequence of the rules is under a fancy update modality that we omit for the sake of presentation.
, that allow us to progress the specification program but without considering the weakest precondition or the left-hand side program.

\frac{\mathsf{specCtx}\quad\mathsf{spec}(K{[}e{]})\quad e\overset{\mathrm{pure}}{\rightsquigarrow}e^{\prime}}{\mathsf{spec}(K{[}e^{\prime}{]})}\quad\textsc{lab={spec-pure}}\frac{\mathsf{specCtx}\quad\mathsf{spec}(K{[}\ell\leftarrow w{]})\quad\ell\mapsto_{\mathsf{s}}v}{\mathsf{spec}(K{[}(){]})\mskip 5.0mu{\ast}\mskip 5.0mu\ell\mapsto_{\mathsf{s}}w}\quad\textsc{lab={spec-store}}

Similarly looking rules exists for all the deterministic right-hand side reductions.

To define
\mathsf{specCtx}
we will use two instances of the
authoritative resource algebra

(
Jung et al. 2015
)
from the Iris ghost theory.
It suffices to know that an instance
F
gives us two resources
F_{\mathord{\bullet}}(a)
and
F_{\mathord{\circ}}(a)
satisfying
F_{\mathord{\bullet}}(a)\mskip 5.0mu{\ast}\mskip 5.0muF_{\mathord{\circ}}(b)\vdash a=b
and that
F_{\mathord{\bullet}}(a)\mskip 5.0mu{\ast}\mskip 5.0muF_{\mathord{\circ}}(b)
can be updated to
F_{\mathord{\bullet}}(a^{\prime})\mskip 5.0mu{\ast}\mskip 5.0muF_{\mathord{\circ}}(a^{\prime})
.
To connect the two parts we will keep
\mathsf{specInterp}_{\mathord{\bullet}}(\rho)
in the specification interpretation
G
(that “lives” in the weakest precondition), and the corresponding
\mathsf{specInterp}_{\mathord{\circ}}(\rho)
in
\mathsf{specCtx}
:

\displaystyle G(\rho)

\displaystyle\triangleq\mathsf{specInterp}_{\mathord{\bullet}}(\rho)

\displaystyle\mathsf{specInv}

\displaystyle\triangleq\exists\rho,e,\sigma,n.\penalty\thinspace\begin{aligned} &\mathsf{specInterp}_{\mathord{\circ}}(\rho)\mskip 5.0mu{\ast}\mskip 5.0mu\mathsf{spec}_{\mathord{\bullet}}(e)\mskip 5.0mu{\ast}\mskip 5.0mu\mathsf{heaps}(\sigma)\mskip 5.0mu{\ast}\mskip 5.0mu\exec_{n}(\rho)(e,\sigma)=1\end{aligned}

\displaystyle\mathsf{specCtx}

\displaystyle\triangleq\hbox to36.26pt{\vbox to11.49pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 18.12775pt\lower-2.74445pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {

\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}{{}}{}

This ensures that the configuration
\rho
tracked in the weakest precondition is the same as the configuration
\rho
tracked in
\mathsf{specCtx}
.
On top of this,
\mathsf{specCtx}
contains resources
\mathsf{spec}_{\mathord{\bullet}}(e)
and
\mathsf{heaps}(\sigma)
while guaranteeing that the configuration
(e,\sigma)
can be reached in
n
deterministic program steps from
\rho
.
The
\mathsf{heaps}(\sigma)
resource gives meaning—using standard Iris ghost theory—to the heap and tape assertions,
\ell\mapsto_{\mathsf{s}}v
and
\iota\hookrightarrow_{\mathsf{s}}(N,\vec{n})
, just like the state interpretation in the weakest precondition.

\exec_{n}:\kern-0.50003pt\textit{Cfg}\to\mathcal{D}(\kern-0.50003pt\textit{Cfg})
denotes the distribution of
n
-step partial execution.
By letting
\mathsf{spec}(e)\triangleq{}\mathsf{spec}_{\mathord{\circ}}(e)
this construction permits the right-hand side program to progress (with deterministic reduction steps) without consideration of the left-hand side as exemplified by
5.1
and
5.1
.
However, when applying coupling rules that actually need to relate the two sides, the proof first “catches up” with
\mathsf{spec}(e)
using the
\mathsf{execCoupl}
rule that progresses the right-hand side independently, before constructing the coupling of interest.

5.2.
Refinement logic

Contextual refinement is a typed relation and hence logical refinement must be typed as well.
To define the refinement logic, we first define a binary value interpretation
\llbracket\tau\rrbracket_{\Delta}
that characterizes the set of pairs of closed values
(v_{1},v_{2})
of type
\tau
such that
v_{1}
contextually refines
v_{2}
.
The definition follows the usual structure of (“logical”) logical relations, see,
e.g.
,
Frumin et al. 2021b
;
Timany et al. 2022
, by structural recursion on
\tau
and uses corresponding logical connectives.
Functions are interpreted via (separating) implication, universal types are interpreted through universal quantification,
etc.
, as found in
Appendix

A
.
The only novelty is the interpretation of the new type of tapes shown below:

\displaystyle\llbracket\mathsf{tape}\rrbracket_{\Delta}(v_{1},v_{2})\triangleq{}\exists\iota_{1},\iota_{2},N.\penalty\thinspace

\displaystyle(v_{1}=\iota_{1})\mskip 5.0mu{\ast}\mskip 5.0mu(v_{2}=\iota_{2})\mskip 5.0mu{\ast}\mskip 5.0mu{}\hbox to102.8pt{\vbox to12.6pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 51.40102pt\lower-3.3pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-50.30103pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{62}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}{{}}{}
{{}}{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -70.99 -3.94 M -70.99 -3.94 L -70.99 12.73 L 70.99 12.73 L 70.99 -3.94 Z M 70.99 12.73}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {{

The interpretation requires that the values are tape labels,
i.e.
, references to tapes, and that they are always empty as captured by the invariant.
Intuitively, this guarantees through coupling rules and the symbolic execution rules from
Figure

23
that we always can couple samplings on these tapes as needed in the compatibility lemma for
3
as discussed in
Section

5.3
.
Point-wise equality of the two tapes would also have been sufficient for the compatibility lemma but by requiring them to be empty we can prove general equivalences such as
\iota:\mathsf{tape}\vdash\operatorname{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{rand}}}(N)\simeq_{\text{ctx}}\operatorname{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{rand}}}(N,\iota):\mathsf{nat}
.

The refinement judgment is defined using the coupling logic in combination with the binary value interpretation.
Recall how the intuitive reading of the refinement judgment
\Delta\vDash_{\mathcal{E}}e_{1}\precsim e_{2}:\tau
is that the expression
e_{1}
refines the expression
e_{2}
at type
\tau
under the invariants in the mask
\mathcal{E}
with interpretations of type variables in
\tau
taken from
\Delta
.
Besides the coupling logic and the binary value interpretations, we will also make use of the resource
\mathsf{naTok}(\mathcal{E})
that keeps track of the set of non-atomic invariants that are currently
closed
.

Putting everything together, the refinement judgment is formally defined as follows:

\displaystyle\Delta\vDash_{\mathcal{E}}e_{1}\precsim e_{2}:\tau\triangleq{}\forall K.\penalty\thinspace

\displaystyle\mathsf{specCtx}\mskip 1.66667mu\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\hbox to10.29pt{\vbox to4.65pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25833pt\lower-2.32639pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}{{}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@roundcap\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 10.43 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{

\lxSVG@closescope
