\displaystyle\mathsf{wp}\penalty\thinspace e_{1}\penalty\thinspace{\left\{v_{1}.\exists v_{2}.\penalty\thinspace\mathsf{spec}(K{[}v_{2}{]})\mskip 5.0mu{\ast}\mskip 5.0mu\mathsf{naTok}(\top)\mskip 5.0mu{\ast}\mskip 5.0mu\llbracket\tau\rrbracket_{\Delta}(v_{1},v_{2})\right\}}

The definition assumes that the right-hand side program is executing
e_{2}
and that the invariants in
\mathcal{E}
are closed, and it concludes that the two executions can be aligned so that if
e_{1}
reduces to some value
v_{1}
then there exists a corresponding execution of
e_{2}
to a value
v_{2}
and all invariants have been closed.
Moreover, the values
v_{1}
and
v_{2}
are related via the binary value interpretation
\llbracket\tau\rrbracket_{\Delta}(v_{1},v_{2})
.
By quantifying over
K
, we close the definition under evaluation contexts on the right-hand side.
For the left-hand side this is not needed as the weakest precondition already satisfies
20
.

5.3.
Soundness

The soundness of the refinement judgment hinges on the soundness of the coupling logic.
The goal of the coupling logic is to show a
coupling
of the execution of the two programs, but to establish a coupling of two distributions they must have the same mass.
Intuitively, due to the approximative nature of step-indexed logics like Clutch, we need to show—at every logical step-index—that a coupling exists, even when the left-hand side program has not yet terminated.
This means we might not have enough mass on the left-hand side to cover all of the mass on the right-hand side.
For this reason we introduce a new notion of
left-partial coupling
.

Definition 4 (Left-Partial Coupling).

Let
\mu_{1}\in\mathcal{D}(A),\mu_{2}\in\mathcal{D}(B)
. A sub-distribution
\mu\in\mathcal{D}(A\times B)
is a
left-partial coupling
of
\mu_{1}
and
\mu_{2}
if

(1)

\forall a.\penalty\thinspace\sum_{b\in B}\mu(a,b)=\mu_{1}(a)

(2)

\forall b.\penalty\thinspace\sum_{a\in A}\mu(a,b)\leq\mu_{2}(b)

Given a relation
R\subseteq A\times B
we say
\mu
is an
R
-left-partial-coupling if furthermore
\supp(\mu)\subseteq R
.
We write
\mu_{1}\lesssim\mu_{2}:R
if there exists an
R
-left-partial-coupling of
\mu_{1}
and
\mu_{2}
.

This means that, for any
\mu\in\mathcal{D}(B)
and any
R\subseteq A\times B
, the zero distribution
\mathbf{0}
trivially satisfies
\mathbf{0}\lesssim\mu:R
.
This reflects the asymmetry of both contextual refinement and our weakest precondition—it allows us to show that a diverging program refines any other program of appropriate type.

Left-partial couplings can also be constructed and composed along the monadic structure of the sub-distribution monad and are implied by regular couplings:

Lemma 5.

If
\mu_{1}\sim\mu_{2}:R
then
\mu_{1}\lesssim\mu_{2}:R
.

Additionally, proving a
(=)
-left-partial-coupling coincides with the point-wise inequality of distributions that will allow us to reason about contextual refinement.

Lemma 6.

If
\mu_{1}\lesssim\mu_{2}:(=)
then
\forall a.\penalty\thinspace\mu_{1}(a)\leq\mu_{2}(a)
.

The
adequacy
theorem of the coupling logic is stated using left-partial couplings.

Theorem 7 (Adequacy).

Let
\varphi:\kern-1.99997pt\textit{Val}\times\kern-1.99997pt\textit{Val}\to\textit{Prop}
be a predicate on values in the meta-logic.
If

\displaystyle\mathsf{specCtx}\mskip 5.0mu{\ast}\mskip 5.0mu\mathsf{spec}(e^{\prime})\vdash\mathsf{wp}\penalty\thinspace e\penalty\thinspace{\left\{v.\exists v^{\prime}.\penalty\thinspace\mathsf{spec}(v^{\prime})\mskip 5.0mu{\ast}\mskip 5.0mu\varphi(v,v^{\prime})\right\}}

is provable in Clutch then
\forall n.\penalty\thinspace\execVal_{n}(e,\sigma)\lesssim\execVal(e^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime}):\varphi
.

As a simple corollary, contextual refinement follows from continuity of
\execVal_{n}
.

The proof of the adequacy theorem goes by induction in both
n
and the
\mathsf{execCoupl}
fixpoint, followed by a case distinction on the big disjunction in the definition of
\mathsf{execCoupl}
.
Most cases are simple coupling compositions along the monadic structure except the cases where we introduce state step couplings that rely on
erasure
in the following sense:

Lemma 8 (Erasure).

If
\sigma_{1}(\iota)\in\mathsf{dom}(\sigma_{1})
then

\displaystyle\execVal_{n}(e_{1},\sigma_{1})\sim(\statestepdistr_{\iota}(\sigma_{1})\mathbin{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\gg\mkern-6.0mu=$}}}\lambda\sigma_{2}.\penalty\thinspace\execVal_{n}(e_{1},\sigma_{2})):(=)

Intuitively, this lemma tells us that we can prepend any program execution with a state step reduction and it will not have an effect on the final result.
The idea behind the proof is that if we append a sampled value
n
to the end of a tape, and if we eventually consume
n
, then we obtain the same distribution as if we never appended
n
in the first place.
This is a property that one should
not
take for granted: the operational semantics has been carefully defined such that reading from an empty tape reduces to a value as well, and none of the other program operations can alter or observe the contents of the tape.
This ensures that presampled values are untouched until consumed and that the proof and the execution is independent.

To show the soundness theorem of the refinement logic, we extend the interpretation of types to typing contexts—
\llbracket\Gamma\rrbracket_{\Delta}(\vec{v},\vec{w})
iff for every
x_{i}\colon\sigma_{i}
in
\Gamma
then
\llbracket\sigma_{i}\rrbracket_{\Delta}(v_{i},w_{i})
holds—and the refinement judgment to open terms by closing substitutions as usual:

\displaystyle\Delta\mid\Gamma\vDash e_{1}\precsim e_{2}:\tau\triangleq{}\forall\vec{v},\vec{w}.\penalty\thinspace\llbracket\Gamma\rrbracket_{\Delta}(\vec{v},\vec{w})\mskip 1.66667mu\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\hbox to10.29pt{\vbox to4.65pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25833pt\lower-2.32639pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}{{}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@roundcap\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 10.43 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{5.0347pt}{-2.32639pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{62}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}}}\mskip 1.66667mu\Delta\vDash e_{1}[\vec{v}/\Gamma]\precsim e_{2}[\vec{w}/\Gamma]:\tau

where
e_{1}[\vec{v}/\Gamma]
denotes simultaneous substitution of every
x_{i}
from
\Gamma
in
e_{1}
by the value
v_{i}
.

We then show, using the structural and symbolic execution rules of the refinement judgment, that the typing rules are
compatible
with the relational interpretation: for every typing rule, if we have a pair of related terms for every premise, then we also have a pair of related terms for the conclusion.
See for instance the compatibility rule for
3
below in the case
\tau=\mathsf{tape}
that follows using
21
and
24
.

\frac{\Delta\mid\Gamma\vDash e_{1}\precsim{e_{1}}^{\prime}:\mathsf{nat}\quad\Delta\mid\Gamma\vDash e_{2}\precsim{e_{2}}^{\prime}:\mathsf{tape}}{\Delta\mid\Gamma\vDash\operatorname{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{rand}}}(e_{1},e_{2})\precsim\operatorname{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{rand}}}({e_{1}}^{\prime},{e_{2}}^{\prime}):\mathsf{nat}}\quad\textsc{lab={rand-compat}}

As a consequence of the compatibility rules, we obtain the fundamental theorem of logical relations.

Theorem 9 (Fundamental theorem).

Let
\Xi\mid\Gamma\vdash e:\tau
be a well-typed term, and let
\Delta
assign a relational interpretation to every type variable in
\Xi
.
Then
\Delta\mid\Gamma\vDash e\precsim e:\tau{}
.

The compatibility rules, moreover, yield that the refinement judgment is a congruence, and together with
Theorem

7
we can then recover contextual refinement:

Theorem 10 (Soundness).

Let
\Xi
be a type variable context, and assume that, for all
\Delta
assigning a relational interpretation to all type variables in
\Xi
, we can derive
\Delta\mid\Gamma\vDash e_{1}\precsim e_{2}:\tau{}
.
Then
\Xi\mid\Gamma\vdash e_{1}\precsim_{\text{ctx}}e_{2}:\tau{}

6.
Case Studies

In the coming sections, we give an overview of some of the example equivalences we have proven with Clutch.
Further details are found in
Appendix

B
and our Coq development.
In particular, in
Appendix

B
we discuss an example by
Sangiorgi and Vignudelli 2016
, which previous probabilistic logical relations without asynchronous couplings could not prove
(
Bizjak 2016
, Sec. 1.5)
.

6.1.
Lazy/eager Coin

In this section we give a more detailed proof of the lazy-eager coin example from
Section

1
.
We will go through the proof step by step but omit the use of
21
and
21
which should be interleaved with the application of most of the mentioned proof rules.

Recall the definitions of
\mathit{lazy}
and
\mathit{eager}
from
Section

1
.
The goal is to show
\vdash\mathit{lazy}\simeq_{\text{ctx}}\mathit{eager}:\mathsf{unit}\to\mathsf{bool}
by first showing
\mathit{lazy}\precsim_{\text{ctx}}\mathit{eager}:\mathsf{unit}\to\mathsf{bool}
and then
\mathit{eager}\precsim_{\text{ctx}}\mathit{lazy}:\mathsf{unit}\to\mathsf{bool}
.

To show
\mathit{lazy}\precsim_{\text{ctx}}\mathit{eager}:\mathsf{unit}\to\mathsf{bool}
, we first define an intermediate labeled version
\mathit{lazy}^{\prime}
of
\mathit{lazy}
(found in
Section

2
).
By transitivity of contextual refinement and
Theorem

1
it is sufficient to show
\vDash\mathit{lazy}\precsim\mathit{lazy}^{\prime}:\mathsf{unit}\to\mathsf{bool}
and
\vDash\mathit{lazy}^{\prime}\precsim\mathit{eager}:\mathsf{unit}\to\mathsf{bool}
.

The first refinement
\vDash\mathit{lazy}\precsim\mathit{lazy}^{\prime}:\mathsf{unit}\to\mathsf{bool}
is mostly straightforward.
By applying
21
followed by
23
and
21
we are left with the goal of proving that the two thunks are related, given
\iota\hookrightarrow_{\mathsf{s}}(1,\epsilon)
,
\ell\mapsto\operatorname{\mathsf{None}}
and
\ell^{\prime}\mapsto_{\mathsf{s}}\operatorname{\mathsf{None}}
for some fresh label
\iota
and fresh locations on the heap
\ell
and
\ell^{\prime}
.
Using
25
we allocate the invariant

\displaystyle\iota\hookrightarrow_{\mathsf{s}}(1,\epsilon)\mskip 5.0mu{\ast}\mskip 5.0mu(

\displaystyle(\ell\mapsto\operatorname{\mathsf{None}}\mskip 5.0mu{\ast}\mskip 5.0mu\ell^{\prime}\mapsto_{\mathsf{s}}\operatorname{\mathsf{None}})

\displaystyle\lor{}(\exists b.\penalty\thinspace\ell\mapsto\operatorname{\mathsf{Some}}(b)\mskip 5.0mu{\ast}\mskip 5.0mu\ell^{\prime}\mapsto_{\mathsf{s}}\operatorname{\mathsf{Some}}(b)))

We continue by

We continue using

\operatorname{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{flip}}}

and
21
to store the fresh bit on the heaps, we close the invariant (now showing the right disjunct as the locations have been updated) using

, and we finish the case using

b
on both sides.

If
\ell
and
\ell^{\prime}
were
not
empty, this is not the first time the function is invoked and we straightforwardly load the same Boolean on both sides using
21
and

and finish the proof using
25
and
21
.

For the second refinement

we start by allocating the tape on the left using

which gives us ownership of a fresh tape
\iota\hookrightarrow(1,\epsilon)

We now couple the
\iota

and the
\operatorname{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{flip}}}()

We continue by allocating the reference on the left using

which gives us some location

Now, we allocate the invariant

\displaystyle(\iota\hookrightarrow(1,b)\mskip 5.0mu{\ast}\mskip 5.0mu\ell\mapsto\operatorname{\mathsf{None}})\lor\ell\mapsto\operatorname{\mathsf{Some}}(b)

\displaystyle\vDash_{\top\setminus\mathcal{N}}\begin{aligned} &\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{let}}\penalty\thinspace b\mathrel{=}\operatorname{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{flip}}}(\iota)\penalty\thinspace\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{in}}\penalty\thinspace\\

The proof of

.

6.2.
ElGamal public key encryption

An encryption scheme is seen as secure if no probabilistic polynomial-time (PPT) adversary
\mathcal{A}
can break it with non-negligible probability.
A common pattern in cryptographic security proofs are security reductions.
To perform a reduction, one assumes that such an adversary
\mathcal{A}
exists, and constructs another PPT adversary
\mathcal{B}
that, using
\mathcal{A}
, solves a computational problem
P
that is believed to be hard.
By contradiction, this means the construction is secure under the assumption that the problem
P
is hard.
A crucial proof step is showing that
\mathcal{B}
together with
P
corresponds to the original construction which can be thought of as the “soundness” of the security reduction.
In this section, we use Clutch to show the soundness of a security reduction of the ElGamal public key encryption scheme
(
Elgamal 1985
)
to the decisional Diffie-Hellman (DDH) computational assumption.

\displaystyle\mathit{keygen}

\displaystyle\triangleq{}\begin{aligned} \lambda\,\_\,.\penalty\thinspace&\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{let}}\penalty\thinspace sk\mathrel{=}\operatorname{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{rand}}}(n)\penalty\thinspace\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{in}}\penalty\thinspace\\
&\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{let}}\penalty\thinspace pk\mathrel{=}g^{sk}\penalty\thinspace\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{in}}\penalty\thinspace\\
&(sk,pk)\end{aligned}

\displaystyle\mathit{dec}

\displaystyle\triangleq{}\lambda\ sk\ (B,X).\penalty\thinspace\ X\cdot{}B^{-sk}

\displaystyle\mathit{enc}\triangleq{}\lambda\ pk\ msg.\penalty\thinspace

\displaystyle\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{let}}\penalty\thinspace b\mathrel{=}\operatorname{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{rand}}}(n)\penalty\thinspace\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{in}}\penalty\thinspace

\displaystyle\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{let}}\penalty\thinspace B\mathrel{=}g^{b}\penalty\thinspace\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{in}}\penalty\thinspace

\displaystyle\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{let}}\penalty\thinspace X\mathrel{=}msg\cdot{}pk^{b}\penalty\thinspace\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{in}}\penalty\thinspace

\displaystyle(B,X)

.
The ElGamal public key scheme.

The ElGamal construction is a public key encryption scheme consisting a tuple of algorithms
(\mathit{keygen},\mathit{enc},\mathit{dec})
whose implementation in
\mathbf{F}_{\mu,\textnormal{ref}}^{\textnormal{rand}}
is shown in
Figure

7
.
The implementation is parameterized by a group
G
which serves to represent messages, ciphertexts, and keys.
We write
G=(1,\,\cdot{}\,,{-}^{-1})
for a finite cyclic group of order
|G|
, generated by
g
, and let
n=|G|-1
.
Intuitively, to show that ElGamal encryption is secure it suffices to show that, given the DDH assumption holds for the group
G
, an adversary
\mathcal{A}
cannot distinguish an encrypted message from a random ciphertext (see,
e.g.
,
(
Rosulek 2020
, §15.3)
).
The DDH assumption for a group
G
says that the two games
\mathit{DH_{real}}
and
\mathit{DH_{rand}}
in
Figure

9
are PPT-indistinguishable which intuitively means that the value
g^{ab}
looks random, even to someone who has seen
g^{a}
and
g^{b}
.

\displaystyle\smash{\mathit{PK}\mathit{{}_{real}}\triangleq{}}

\displaystyle\smash{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{let}}\penalty\thinspace(sk,pk)\mathrel{=}\mathit{keygen}{}()\penalty\thinspace\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{in}}\penalty\thinspace}

\displaystyle\smash{\quad\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{if}}\penalty\thinspace\mathop{!}{count}\neq 0\penalty\thinspace\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{then}}\penalty\thinspace}

Figure 8
.
Public key security.

The intuitive notion of encryption scheme security can be made precise
5
5

5

Several formulations exist in the literature; we take inspiration from the textbook presentation of
Rosulek 2020
.
as the indistinguishability of two security games,
i.e.
, stylized interactions,
\mathit{PK}\mathit{{}_{real}}
and
\mathit{PK}\mathit{{}_{rand}}
shown in
Figure

8
, by a PPT
6
6

6

Polynomial-time with respect to the security parameter,
i.e.
the logarithm of the size of the group for ElGamal.
adversary.
Here we interpret the notion of an “adversary” as a program context.
Both security games are initialised by generating a secret/public-key pair
(sk,pk)
, of which
pk
is returned to the adversary (the context).
The adversary gets to examine the public key and an “encryption oracle”
\mathit{query}
,
i.e.
, a partial application of the encryption function specialized to a particular key.
The difference between
\mathit{PK}\mathit{{}_{real}}
and
\mathit{PK}\mathit{{}_{rand}}
lies in the
\mathit{query}
function.
While
\mathit{PK}\mathit{{}_{real}}
encrypts the message
msg
provided as input,
\mathit{PK}\mathit{{}_{rand}}
instead returns a randomly sampled ciphertext.
Both games use a counter
count
to ensure that the
\mathit{query}
oracle can be called only once.
One attempt at distinguishing the security games will thus correspond exactly to one attempt at distinguishing
\mathit{DH_{real}}
from
\mathit{DH_{rand}}
.

\displaystyle\mathit{DH_{real}}\triangleq{}

\displaystyle\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{let}}\penalty\thinspace a\mathrel{=}\operatorname{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{rand}}}(n)\penalty\thinspace\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{in}}\penalty\thinspace

\displaystyle\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{let}}\penalty\thinspace b\mathrel{=}\operatorname{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{rand}}}(n)\penalty\thinspace\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{in}}\penalty\thinspace

\displaystyle(g^{a},g^{b},g^{ab})

\displaystyle\mathit{DH_{rand}}\triangleq{}

\displaystyle\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{let}}\penalty\thinspace a\mathrel{=}\operatorname{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{rand}}}(n)\penalty\thinspace\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{in}}\penalty\thinspace

\displaystyle\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{let}}\penalty\thinspace b\mathrel{=}\operatorname{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{rand}}}(n)\penalty\thinspace\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{in}}\penalty\thinspace

\displaystyle\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{let}}\penalty\thinspace c\mathrel{=}\operatorname{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{rand}}}(n)\penalty\thinspace\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{in}}\penalty\thinspace

\displaystyle(g^{a},g^{b},g^{c})

Figure 9
.
The Decisional Diffie-Hellman game.

The idea now is to use Clutch as a step towards reducing indistinguishability of
\mathit{PK}\mathit{{}_{real}}
and
\mathit{PK}\mathit{{}_{rand}}
to the indistinguishability of
\mathit{DH_{real}}
and
\mathit{DH_{rand}}
.
Specifically, we will exhibit a context
\mathcal{C}
and show

(1)

\displaystyle\vdash\mathit{PK}\mathit{{}_{real}}\simeq_{\text{ctx}}\mathcal{C}[\mathit{DH_{real}}]:\tau{}_{\mathit{\mathit{PK}}}

(2)

\displaystyle\vdash\mathit{PK}\mathit{{}_{rand}}\simeq_{\text{ctx}}\mathcal{C}[\mathit{DH_{rand}}]:\tau{}_{\mathit{\mathit{PK}}}

Then we can complete the reduction on paper (outside of Clutch) as follows.
7
7

7

To mechanize the argument one would need to formalize a notion of PPT and a proof that the context is in fact PPT which is out of scope for the work at hand.

To prove that the DDH assumption implies public key security, we assume the contrapositive,
i.e.
, that there exists an adversarial context
\mathcal{A}
that can distinguish
\mathit{PK}\mathit{{}_{real}}
from
\mathit{PK}\mathit{{}_{rand}}
.
Using
1
and
2
we then get that
\mathcal{A}
can distinguish
\mathcal{C}[\mathit{DH_{real}}]
from
\mathcal{C}[\mathit{DH_{rand}}]
.
But this means that
\mathcal{A}[\mathcal{C}[-]]
is a context that can distinguish the DDH games, and hence contradicts our assumption, if
\mathcal{A}[\mathcal{C}[-]]
is PPT.
The context
\mathcal{C}
for
1
and
2
is given by
Figure

8
8(b)
(note that the hole is in the first line).
The proof that
\mathcal{A}[\mathcal{C}[-]]
is PPT if
\mathcal{A}[-]
is PPT is outside of the scope of Clutch.

We will only focus on the first equation (
1
), since the proof of (
2
) is similar.
The proof proceeds via an intermediate program,
\mathit{PK}^{\mathit{tape}}_{\mathit{real}}
, which differs from
\mathit{PK}\mathit{{}_{real}}
only in that the random sampling in
query
is labelled with the tape
\beta
.
By transitivity, it suffices to show that
\vdash\mathit{PK}\mathit{{}_{real}}\simeq_{\text{ctx}}\mathit{PK}^{\mathit{tape}}_{\mathit{real}}:\tau
and
\vdash\mathit{PK}^{\mathit{tape}}_{\mathit{real}}\simeq_{\text{ctx}}\mathcal{C}[\mathit{DH_{real}}]:\tau
, as displayed in
Figure

10
.
The first equivalence is trivial.
The essential difference between
\mathit{PK}^{\mathit{tape}}_{\mathit{real}}
and
\mathcal{C}[\mathit{DH_{real}}]
is that the
\mathit{query}
function in
\mathit{PK}^{\mathit{tape}}_{\mathit{real}}
samples
b
lazily, whereas in
\mathcal{C}[\mathit{DH_{real}}]
, the sampling of
b
occurs eagerly in the beginning.
The proof now proceeds in a manner similar to the lazy-eager coin example; details can be found in the formalization.

Clutch is well-suited for proving the soundness of the reduction for two reasons.
Firstly, any public key encryption scheme can only be secure if it employs randomized encryption
(
Goldwasser and Micali 1984
)
.
Dealing with randomization is thus unavoidable.
Secondly, reasoning about the encryption oracle involves moving the random sampling used in the encryption across a function boundary (the
\mathit{query}
oracle) as we saw.
This part of the argument crucially relies on asynchronous couplings.
Systems like EasyCrypt and CertiCrypt handle this part of the argument through special-purpose rules for swapping statements that allows moving the random sampling outside the function boundary.
However, it crucially relies on the fact that these works consider first-order languages with global state and use syntactic criteria and assertions on memory disjointness.

Note moreover that our security formulation makes crucial use of the fact that
\mathbf{F}_{\mu,\textnormal{ref}}^{\textnormal{rand}}
is higher-order, randomized, and supports local state to return the
\mathit{query}
closure as a first class value.
This allows us to capture the textbook cryptographic notion of adversaries and of a (closed-box) “oracle” precisely using standard notions such as higher-order functions and contextual equivalence, without introducing special linguistic and logical categories of adversaries parameterized by a set of oracles.

\mathit{PK}\mathit{{}_{real}}

\simeq_{\text{ctx}}

\displaystyle\smash{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{let}}\penalty\thinspace sk\mathrel{=}\operatorname{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{rand}}}(n)\penalty\thinspace\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{in}}\penalty\thinspace}

\displaystyle\smash{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{let}}\penalty\thinspace pk\mathrel{=}g^{sk}\penalty\thinspace\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{in}}\penalty\thinspace}

\displaystyle\smash{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{let}}\penalty\thinspace count\mathrel{=}\operatorname{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{ref}}}0\penalty\thinspace\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{in}}\penalty\thinspace}

\displaystyle\smash{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{let}}\penalty\thinspace\mathit{query}=\lambda\ msg.\penalty\thinspace}

\displaystyle\smash{\quad\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{if}}\penalty\thinspace\mathop{!}{count}\neq 0\penalty\thinspace\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{then}}\penalty\thinspace}

\displaystyle\smash{\quad\quad\operatorname{\mathsf{None}}}

\displaystyle\smash{\quad\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{else}}\penalty\thinspace}

\displaystyle\smash{\quad\quad count\leftarrow 1;}

\displaystyle\smash{\quad\quad\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{let}}\penalty\thinspace b=\operatorname{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{rand}}}(n)\penalty\thinspace\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{in}}\penalty\thinspace}

The “real” direction of the security reduction.

6.3.
Hash functions

When analyzing data structures that use hash functions, one commonly
models the hash function under the

uniform hash assumption
or the
random oracle model

(
Bellare and Rogaway 1993
)
.
That is, a hash function
h
from a set of keys
K
to values
V

behaves as if, for each key
k
, the hash
h(k)
is randomly sampled
from a uniform distribution over
V
, independently of all the other
keys. Of course, hash functions are not known to satisfy
this assumption perfectly, but it can nevertheless be a useful modeling
assumption for analyzing programs that use hashes.

The function
\mathit{eager\_hash}
in
Figure

11
encodes
such a model of hash functions in
\mathbf{F}_{\mu,\textnormal{ref}}^{\textnormal{rand}}{}
. (We explain
the reason for the “eager” name later.) Given a
non-negative integer
n
, executing
\mathit{eager\_hash}\ n
returns a hash
function with
K=\{0,\dots,n\}
and
V=\mathbb{B}
.
To do so, it initializes a mutable map
m
and then calls
\mathit{sample\_all}
,
which samples a Boolean
b
with
\operatorname{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{flip}}}
for each key
k
and stores the results in
m
.
These Booleans serve as the hash values.
On input
k
, the hash function returned by
\mathit{eager\_hash}
looks up
k
in the map
m

and returns the result, with a default value of
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{false}}
if
k\not\in K
.

\displaystyle\mathit{eager\_hash}\triangleq{}

\displaystyle\quad\lambda n.\penalty\thinspace\begin{aligned} &\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{let}}\penalty\thinspace m\mathrel{=}\mathit{init\_map}\ ()\penalty\thinspace\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{in}}\penalty\thinspace\\
&\mathit{sample\_all}\ m\ (n+1);\\
&(\lambda k.\penalty\thinspace{\begin{array}[t]{rll}\lx@intercol\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{match}}\penalty\thinspace\mathit{get}\ m\ k\penalty\thinspace\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{with}}\hfil\lx@intercol\\
&\operatorname{\mathsf{Some}}(b)&\Rightarrow b\\
|&\operatorname{\mathsf{None}}&\Rightarrow\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{false}}\\
\lx@intercol\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{end}})\hfil\lx@intercol\end{array}}{}\end{aligned}

\displaystyle\mathit{lazy\_hash}\triangleq{}

\displaystyle\quad\lambda n.\penalty\thinspace\begin{aligned} &\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{let}}\penalty\thinspace vm\mathrel{=}\mathit{init\_map}\ ()\penalty\thinspace\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{in}}\penalty\thinspace\\
&\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{let}}\penalty\thinspace tm\mathrel{=}\mathit{init\_map}\ ()\penalty\thinspace\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{in}}\penalty\thinspace\\
&\mathit{alloc\_tapes}\ tm\ (n+1);\\
&(\lambda k.\penalty\thinspace{\begin{array}[t]{rll}\lx@intercol\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{match}}\penalty\thinspace\mathit{get}\ vm\ k\penalty\thinspace\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{with}}\hfil\lx@intercol\\
&\operatorname{\mathsf{Some}}(b)&\Rightarrow b\\
|&\operatorname{\mathsf{None}}&\Rightarrow{{\begin{array}[t]{rll}\lx@intercol\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{match}}\penalty\thinspace\mathit{get}\ tm\ k\penalty\thinspace\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{with}}\hfil\lx@intercol\\
&\operatorname{\mathsf{Some}}(\iota)\Rightarrow\\
&\quad{\begin{array}[t]{l}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{let}}\penalty\thinspace b\mathrel{=}\operatorname{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{flip}}}(\iota)\penalty\thinspace\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{in}}\\
\mathit{set}\ vm\ b;b\end{array}}\\
|&\operatorname{\mathsf{None}}\Rightarrow\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{false}}\\
\lx@intercol\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{end}}\hfil\lx@intercol\end{array}}}\\
\lx@intercol\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{end}})\hfil\lx@intercol\end{array}}\end{aligned}

Figure 11
.
Eager and lazy models of hash functions.

However, this model of uniform hash functions can be inconvenient for
proofs because all of the random hash values are sampled

eagerly
when the function is initialized. To overcome this, an important
technique in pencil-and-paper proofs is to show that the hash values
can be sampled
lazily
(see,
e.g.
,
Mittelbach and Fischlin 2021
). That is, we only sample a
key
k
’s hash value when it is hashed for the first time. This lets
us more conveniently couple that sampling step with some step in
another program.

Motivated by applications to proofs in cryptography,
Almeida et al. 2019
formalized in EasyCrypt a proof of equivalence between an eager and lazy random oracle.
Although sufficient for their intended application, this proof was done in the context of a language that uses syntactic restrictions to model the hash function’s private state.
To the best of our knowledge, no such equivalence proof between lazy and eager sampling has previously been given for a language with higher-order state and general references.

As an application of Clutch, we prove such an equivalence in
\mathbf{F}_{\mu,\textnormal{ref}}^{\textnormal{rand}}
.
The function
\mathit{lazy\_hash}
shown in
Figure

11
encodes
the lazy sampling version of the random hash generator. For its
internal state, the lazy hash uses two mutable maps: the tape map
tm
stores
tapes to be used for random sampling, and the value map
vm
stores
the previously sampled values for keys that have been hashed. After
initializing these maps, it calls
\mathit{alloc\_tapes}
, which allocates a
tape for each key
k\in K
and stores the associated tape in
tm
,
but does not yet sample hashes for any keys. The hash function
returned by
\mathit{lazy\_hash}
determines the hash for a key
k
in two
stages. It first looks up
k
in
vm
to see if
k
already has a
previously sampled hash value, and if so, returns the found
value. Otherwise, it looks up
k
in the tape map
tm
. If no tape is
found, then
k
must not be in
K
, so the function returns

\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{false}}
. If a tape
\iota
is found, then the code samples a Boolean

b
from this tape with
\operatorname{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{flip}}}
, stores
b
for the key
k
in
vm
,
and then returns
b
.

We prove that the eager and lazy versions are contextually equivalent, that is,

\vdash\mathit{eager\_hash}\ n\simeq_{\text{ctx}}\mathit{lazy\_hash}\ n:\mathsf{int}\rightarrow\mathsf{bool}
.
The core idea behind this contextual equivalence proof is to maintain
an invariant between the internal state of the two hash
functions. Let
m
be the internal map used by the eager hash and let

tm
and
vm
be the tape and value maps, respectively, for the lazy
hash. Then, at a high level, the invariant maintains the following
properties:

(1)

\mathsf{dom}(m)=\mathsf{dom}(tm)=\{0,\dots,n\}
.

(2)

For all
k\in\{0,\dots,n\}
, if
m[k]=b
then either

(a)

vm[k]=b
, or

(b)

vm[k]=\bot
and
tm[k]=\iota
for some tape label
\iota
such that
\iota\hookrightarrow(1,b)
.

Case (a) and (b) of the second part of this invariant capture the two possible states each key
k
can be in.
Either the hash of
k
has been looked up before (case a), and so the sampled value stored in
vm
must match that of
m
, or it has not been looked up (case b) and the tape for the key must contain the same value as
m[k]
for its next value.

To establish this invariant when the hashes are initialized, we
asynchronously couple the eager hash function’s
\operatorname{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{flip}}}
for key
k

with a tape step for the tape
\iota
associated with
k
in the lazy
table. The invariant ensures that the values returned by the two hash
functions will be the same when a key
k
is queried. The cases of
the invariant correspond to the branches of the lazy function’s
match statements: if the key
k
is in
K
and has been queried
before, the maps will return the same values found in
m
and
vm
. If
it has not been queried before, then
\operatorname{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{flip}}}
in the lazy
version will sample the value on the tape for the key, which matches

m[k]
. Moreover, the update that writes this sampled value to
vm

preserves the invariant, switching from case (b) to case (a) for
the queried key.

We have used this more convenient lazy encoding to verify examples that use hash functions.
For instance, one scheme to implement random number generators is to use a cryptographic hash function
(
Barker and Kelsey 2015
)
.
The program
\mathit{init\_hash\_rng}
in
Figure

12(a)
implements a simplified version of such a scheme.

\displaystyle\mathit{init\_hash\_rng}\triangleq{}

\displaystyle\quad\lambda\_.\penalty\thinspace\begin{aligned} &\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{let}}\penalty\thinspace f\mathrel{=}\mathit{lazy\_hash}\ \texttt{MAX}\penalty\thinspace\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{in}}\penalty\thinspace\\
&\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{let}}\penalty\thinspace c\mathrel{=}\operatorname{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{ref}}}{0}\penalty\thinspace\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{in}}\penalty\thinspace\\
&(\lambda\_.\penalty\thinspace\begin{aligned} &\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{let}}\penalty\thinspace n\mathrel{=}\mathop{!}{c}\penalty\thinspace\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{in}}\penalty\thinspace\\
&\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{let}}\penalty\thinspace b\mathrel{=}f\ n\penalty\thinspace\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{in}}\penalty\thinspace\\
&c\leftarrow n+1;b)\end{aligned}\end{aligned}

(a)

Hashing random number generator.

\displaystyle\mathit{init\_bounded\_rng}\triangleq{}

\displaystyle\quad\lambda\_.\penalty\thinspace\begin{aligned} &\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{let}}\penalty\thinspace c\mathrel{=}\operatorname{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{ref}}}{0}\penalty\thinspace\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{in}}\penalty\thinspace\\
&(\lambda\_.\penalty\thinspace\begin{aligned} &\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{let}}\penalty\thinspace n\mathrel{=}\mathop{!}{c}\penalty\thinspace\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{in}}\penalty\thinspace\\
&\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{let}}\penalty\thinspace b\mathrel{=}\begin{aligned} &\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{if}}\penalty\thinspace n\leq\texttt{MAX}\penalty\thinspace\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{then}}\penalty\thinspace\operatorname{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{flip}}}()\\
&\penalty\thinspace\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{else}}\penalty\thinspace\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{false}}\penalty\thinspace\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{in}}\penalty\thinspace\\
\end{aligned}\\
&c\leftarrow n+1;b)\end{aligned}\end{aligned}

(b)

Bounded random number generator.

Figure 12
.
Random number generators.

When run,
\mathit{init\_hash\_rng}
generates a lazy hash function
f
for the key space
K=\{0,\dots,\texttt{MAX}\}

for some fixed constant
MAX
. It also allocates a counter
c
as a reference initialized to
0
.
It returns a sampling function, let us call it
h
, that uses
f
and
c
to generate random Booleans. Each time

h
is called, it loads the current value
n
from
c
and hashes
n
with
f
to get
a Boolean
b
. It then increments
c
and returns the Boolean
b
.
Repeated calls to
h
return independent, uniformly sampled Booleans, so long as we make no more than
MAX
calls.

We prove that
\mathit{init\_hash\_rng}
is contextually equivalent to a “bounded” random number generator
\mathit{init\_bounded\_rng}
in
Figure

12(b)
that directly calls
\operatorname{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{flip}}}
.
The proof works by showing that, so long as
n\leq\texttt{MAX}
, then each time a sample is generated,
the value of
n
will not have been hashed
before. Thus, we may couple the random hash value with the
\operatorname{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{flip}}}
call in
\mathit{init\_bounded\_rng}
.
This argument relies on the fact that the counter
c
is private, encapsulated
state, which is easy to reason about using the relational judgment since Clutch is a separation logic.

6.4.
Lazily sampled big integers

Certain randomized data structures, such as treaps
(
Seidel and Aragon 1996
)
,
need to generate random
priorities
as operations are
performed. One can view these priorities as an
abstract data type equipped with a total order supporting two operations: (1) a
sample
function
that randomly generates a new priority according to some distribution, and (2) a
comparison
operation
that takes a pair of priorities
(p_{1},p_{2})
and returns
-1
(if
p_{1}<p_{2}
),
0
(if
p_{1}=p_{2})
, or
1
(if
p_{2}<p_{1}
).
The full details of how priorities are used in such data structures are not relevant
here. Instead, what is important to know is that it is ideal to avoid

collisions
, that is, sampling the same priority multiple
times.

A simple way to implement priorities is to represent them as integers sampled from some fixed set
\{0,\dots,n\}
.
However, to minimize collisions, we may need to make
n
very large.
But making
n
large has a cost, because then priorities requires more random bits to generate and more space to store.
An alternative is to
lazily
sample the integer that represents the priority.
Because we only need to compare priorities, we can delay sampling bits of the integer until they are needed to resolve ties during comparisons.
A lazily-sampled integer can be encoded as a pair of a tape label
\iota
and a linked list of length at most
N
, where each node in the list represents a
digit
of the integer in base
B
, with the head of the list being the most significant digit.

In
Appendix

B
, we describe such an implementation of lazily-sampled integers, with
N=8
and
B=2^{32}
. Our Coq development contains a proof that this implementation is contextually
equivalent to code that eagerly samples a 256-bit integer by bit-shifting and adding
8 32-bit integers. Crucially, this contextual equivalence is at an
abstract
existential type
\tau
. Specifically,
we define the type of abstract priorities

\tau\triangleq\exists\alpha.\penalty\thinspace(\mathsf{unit}\rightarrow\alpha)\times((\alpha\times\alpha)\rightarrow\mathsf{int})
.
Then we have the equivalence

\vdash(\mathit{sample\_lazy\_int},\mathit{cmp\_lazy})\simeq_{\text{ctx}}(\mathit{sample256},\mathit{cmp}):\tau

where
\mathit{cmp}
is just primitive integer comparison.
The proof uses tapes to presample the bits of the lazy integer and couples
these with the eager version. The
\mathit{cmp\_lazy}
function traverses
and mutates the linked lists representing the integers being compared, which separation logic
is well-suited for reasoning about.

7.
Counterexamples

This section justifies some design choices in Clutch by presenting counterexamples showing the unsoundness of two variants of the logic.
In the first counterexample, we show that annotating sampling statements with tape labels is needed in our current formulation of the logic, since their omission leads to unsoundness.
In the second, we show that combining prophecy variables
(
Jung et al. 2020
)
with the usual coupling rules of pRHL (without presampling) is unsound, implying that presampling cannot somehow be implemented in terms of prophecy variables.

7.1.
Syntactic restriction on presampling

One may wonder whether it is necessary for tapes and labels to appear in the program and program state, but they do in fact play a subtle yet crucial role.
Consider the following program
\mathit{flip\_or}
that applies a logical disjunction to two fresh samples:

\displaystyle\mathit{flip\_or}\triangleq{}

\displaystyle\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{let}}\penalty\thinspace x\mathrel{=}\operatorname{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{flip}}}()\penalty\thinspace\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{in}}\penalty\thinspace

\displaystyle\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{let}}\penalty\thinspace y\mathrel{=}\operatorname{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{flip}}}()\penalty\thinspace\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{in}}\penalty\thinspace

\displaystyle x\mathbin{||}y

and compare it to the program
\mathit{flip}\triangleq{}\operatorname{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{flip}}}()
that just samples a bit.
These two programs are obviously
not
contextually equivalent: with probability
3/4
the program
\mathit{flip\_or}
will return
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{true}}
whereas the program
\mathit{flip}
only does so with probability
1/2
.
Yet, if we introduce a rule for
\operatorname{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{flip}}}
that could draw from
any
presampling tape (
i.e.
, without requiring sampling statements to be annotated with the tape they will draw from), the logic would allow one to “prove” that they are equivalent.

Assume the following (unsound!) rule

\frac{\iota\hookrightarrow(1,b\cdot\vec{b})\quad\iota\hookrightarrow(1,\vec{b})\mskip 1.66667mu\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\hbox to10.29pt{\vbox to4.65pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25833pt\lower-2.32639pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}{{}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@roundcap\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 10.43 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{5.0347pt}{-2.32639pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{62}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}}}\mskip 1.66667mu\Delta\vDash_{\mathcal{E}}K{[}b{]}\precsim e_{2}:\tau}{\Delta\vDash_{\mathcal{E}}K{[}\operatorname{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{flip}}}(){]}\precsim e_{2}:\tau}\quad\textsc{lab={rel-tape-unsound}}

that says that when sampling on the left-hand side, we may instead draw a bit
b
from
some
prover-chosen presampling tape
\iota
.
To see why this rule cannot be sound, we will show
\vDash\mathit{flip}\precsim\mathit{flip\_or}:\mathsf{bool}
.

First, we introduce two tapes with resources
\iota_{1}\hookrightarrow(1,\epsilon)
and
\iota_{2}\hookrightarrow(1,\epsilon)
on the left-hand side, either explicitly allocated in code as in Clutch or as pure ghost resources, if that is possible in our hypothetical logic.
Second, we couple the tape
\iota_{1}
with the
x
-sampling and
\iota_{2}
with the
y
-sampling using
24
such that we end up with
\iota_{1}\hookrightarrow(1,b_{1})
and
\iota_{2}\hookrightarrow(1,b_{2})
and the goal
\vDash\operatorname{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{flip}}}()\precsim b_{1}\mathbin{||}b_{2}:\mathsf{bool}
.
Finally, we do a case distinction on both
b_{1}
and
b_{2}
: if both are
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{true}}
, or both are
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{false}}
, it does not matter which tape we use when applying
7.1
.
If, on the other hand, only
b_{i}
is
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{true}}
, we choose
\iota_{i}
and apply
7.1
which finishes the proof.

The crucial observation is that by labeling tapes in the program syntax, however, we prevent
the prover
from doing case analysis on presampled values to decide which tape to read—the syntax will dictate which tape to use and hence which value to read.
Concretely, in
\mathbf{F}_{\mu,\textnormal{ref}}^{\textnormal{rand}}
, unlabeled
\operatorname{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{flip}}}
s always reduce uniformly at random and only labeled sampling statements will read from presampling tapes which prevents us from proving the unsound
7.1
.

Besides motivating why soundly allowing presampling is subtle, this counterexample also emphasizes why the fact that labels appear in the program and in the program syntax is important.
We do not claim that these annotations are absolutely necessary for some kind of presampling to be sound, as some very different formulation of the logic might be able to avoid them, but like for
prophecy variables

(
Jung et al. 2020
)
where similar “ghost information” is needed in the actual program code, it is not obvious how to do without it.
We remind the reader that presampling tapes nevertheless remain a proof-device as tapes can be erased through refinement as discussed in
Section

2
.

7.2.
Incompatibility with prophecy variables

Presampling tapes bear
some
resemblance to prophecy variables in that they give us the means to talk about the future.
However, prophecy variables, as previously developed in the context of Iris
(
Jung et al. 2020
)
, are unsound for the (synchronous) coupling logic as illustrated below.

Assume the existence of two operators
\operatorname{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{NewProph}}}
and
\operatorname{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{Resolve}}}p\operatorname{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{to}}}b
in our programming language and their (unsound for Clutch!) Hoare-triple specifications found below.

\frac{}{{\left\{\mathsf{True}\right\}}\penalty\thinspace\operatorname{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{NewProph}}}\penalty\thinspace{\left\{p.\exists b\in\mathbb{B}.\operatorname{\mathsf{Proph}}(p,b)\right\}}}\quad\textsc{lab={wp-newproph-unsound}}\par\frac{}{{\left\{\operatorname{\mathsf{Proph}}(p,b)\ast b^{\prime}\in\mathbb{B}\right\}}\penalty\thinspace\operatorname{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{Resolve}}}p\operatorname{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{to}}}b^{\prime}\penalty\thinspace{\left\{b=b^{\prime}\right\}}}\quad\textsc{lab={wp-resolve-unsound}}

The specifications give us access to
Boolean one-shot prophecies

(
Jung et al. 2020
)
.

\operatorname{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{NewProph}}}
allocates a fresh prophecy variable
p
and a resource
\operatorname{\mathsf{Proph}}(p,b)
that tracks its future resolution
b
.
Given ownership of
\operatorname{\mathsf{Proph}}(p,b)
then
\operatorname{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{Resolve}}}p\operatorname{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{to}}}b^{\prime}
resolves the prophecy variable
p
to a value
b^{\prime}
and knowledge that
b=b^{\prime}
was the case all along.
To see why these operations and rules cannot be sound in the coupling logic, we will show
\vDash\mathit{flip\_proph}\precsim\mathit{flip}:\mathsf{bool}
where

\displaystyle\mathit{flip\_proph}\triangleq{}

\displaystyle\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{let}}\penalty\thinspace p\mathrel{=}\operatorname{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{NewProph}}}\penalty\thinspace\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{in}}\penalty\thinspace

\displaystyle\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{let}}\penalty\thinspace x\mathrel{=}\operatorname{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{flip}}}()\penalty\thinspace\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{in}}\penalty\thinspace

\displaystyle\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{let}}\penalty\thinspace y\mathrel{=}\operatorname{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{flip}}}()\penalty\thinspace\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{in}}\penalty\thinspace

\displaystyle\hskip-1.49994pt\operatorname{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{Resolve}}}p\operatorname{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{to}}}y;

\displaystyle x\mathbin{\&\&}y

which cannot be the case as
\mathit{flip\_proph}
returns
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{true}}
only with probability
1/4
.

We unfold the relational judgment and apply
7.2
which gives us a prophecy about
y
and its future resolution
b
.
If
b
is true, the evaluation on the left is predetermined to be
x\mathbin{\&\&}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{true}}=x
.
By coupling the sampling of
x
with the
\operatorname{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{flip}}}()
on the right using
24
, we finish using
23
and
7.2
.
On the other hand, if
b
is false, the evaluation on the left is predetermined to be
x\mathbin{\&\&}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{false}}=\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{false}}
.
We apply
23
first and couple the sampling of
y
with the
\operatorname{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{flip}}}()
on the right using
24
and finish using
7.2
.

The counterexample shows that prophecy variables are unsound for the coupling logic, for the same reason that presampling is unsound without syntactic tape labels: If the prover can predict the outcomes of random samples ahead of time, it gives them too much power to choose which sampling they couple with.

8.
Coq Formalization

All the results presented in the paper, including the background on probability theory, the formalization of the logic, and the case studies have been formalized in the Coq proof assistant
(
The Coq Development Team 2022
)
.
The results about probability theory are built on top of the Coquelicot library
(
Boldo et al. 2015
)
, extending their results to real series indexed by countable types.

Although we build our logic on top of Iris
(
Jung et al. 2018
)
, significant work is involved in formalizing the operational semantics of probabilistic languages, our new notion of weakest precondition that internalizes the coupling-based reasoning, and the erasure theorem that allows us to conclude the existence of a coupling.
Our development integrates smoothly with the Iris Proof Mode
(
Krebbers et al. 2017b
)
and we have adapted much of the tactical support from ReLoC
(
Frumin et al. 2021b
)
to reason about the relational judgment.

9.
Related Work

Separation logic

Relational separation logics have been developed on top of Iris for a range of properties, such as contextual refinement
(
Frumin et al. 2021b
;
Krebbers et al. 2017b
;
Timany et al. 2018
;
Timany and Birkedal 2019
)
, simulation
(
Chajed et al. 2019
;
Timany et al. 2021
;
Gäher et al. 2022
)
, and security
(
Frumin et al. 2021a
;
Gregersen et al. 2021
;
Georges et al. 2022
)
.
The representation of the right-hand side program as a resource is a recurring idea, but our technical construction with run ahead is novel.
With the exception of
Tassarotti and Harper 2019
, probabilistic languages have not been considered in Iris.

Tassarotti and Harper 2019
develop a logic to show refinement between a probabilistic program and a semantic model, not a program.
The logic relies on couplings, but it requires synchronization of sampling.

In
Batz et al. 2019
, a framework in which logical assertions are functions ranging over the non-negative reals is presented.
The connectives of separation logic are given an interpretation as maps from pairs of non-negative reals to the positive reals.
This work focuses on proving quantitative properties of a single program,
e.g.
, bounding the probability that certain events happen.
A variety of works have developed separation logics in which the separating conjunction models various forms of probabilistic independence
(
Barthe et al. 2020
;
Bao et al. 2021
;
Bao et al. 2022
)
.
For example, the statement
P\mskip 5.0mu{\ast}\mskip 5.0muQ
is taken to mean “the distribution of
P
is independent from the distribution of
Q
”.

Prophecy variables
(
Abadi and Lamport 1988
;
Abadi and Lamport 1991
)
have been integrated into separation logic in both unary
(
Jung et al. 2020
)
and relational settings
(
Frumin et al. 2021b
)
.
The technical solution uses program annotations and physical state reminiscent of our construction with presampling tapes, but prophecy resolution is a physical program step, whereas presampling in our work is a logical operation.
Prophecies can also be erased through refinement
(
Frumin et al. 2021b
)
.

Probabilistic couplings

Probabilistic couplings are a technique from probability theory that can be used to prove equivalences between distributions or mixing times of Markov chains
(
Aldous 1983
)
.
In computer science, they have been used to reason about relational properties of programs such as equivalences
(
Barthe et al. 2015
)
and differential privacy
(
Barthe et al. 2016a
)
.
However, these logics requires the sampling points on both programs to be synchronized in order to construct couplings.
In a higher-order setting, the logic by
Aguirre et al. 2018
establish so-called “shift couplings” between probabilistic streams that evolve at different rates, but these rules are ad-hoc and limited to the stream type.
Also in the higher-order setting,
Aguirre et al. 2021
use couplings to reason about adversarially-defined properties, however they only support synchronous couplings, first-order global state, and use a graded state monad to enforce separation of adversary memories.

Logical relations

Step-indexed logical relations have been applied to reason about contextual equivalence of probabilistic programs in a variety of settings.

Bizjak and Birkedal 2015
develop logical relations for a language similar to ours, although only with first-order state.
This work has since been extended to a language with continuous probabilistic choice (but without state and impredicative polymorphism)
(
Wand et al. 2018
)
, for which equivalence is shown by establishing a measure preserving transformation between the sources of randomness for both programs.
Recently, this was further extended to support nested inference queries
(
Zhang and Amin 2022
)
.

Another line of work
(
Dal Lago and Gavazzo 2021
;
Dal Lago and Gavazzo 2022
)
uses so called differential logical relations to reason about contextual distance rather than equivalence.
Programs are related using metrics rather than equivalence relations, which allows to quantify how similar programs are.

Cryptographic frameworks

CertiCrypt
(
Barthe et al. 2010
;
Barthe et al. 2009
)
is a framework for cryptographic game-playing proofs written in a simple probabilistic first-order while-language (“pWhile”).
CertiCrypt formalizes a denotational semantics for pWhile in Coq and supports reasoning about the induced notion of program equivalence via a pRHL, and provides dedicated tactics for lazy/eager sampling transformations.
These kind of transformations are non-trivial for expressive languages like ours.
CertiCrypt also provides a quantitative unary logic.

EasyCrypt
(
Barthe et al. 2013
)
is a standalone prover for higher-order logic building on CertiCrypt’s ideas. It leverages the first-order nature of pWhile for proof automation via SMT solvers.
EasyCrypt extends pWhile with a module system
(
Barbosa et al. 2021
)
to support reasoning about abstract code as module parameters.
It integrates a quantitative unary logic with pRHL, and supports reasoning about complexity in terms of oracle calls
(
Barbosa et al. 2021
)
.
Both automation and these kind of properties are out of scope for our work but would be interesting future directions.

In FCF
(
Petcher and Morrisett 2015
)
, programs are written as Coq expressions in the free subdistributions monad.
Proofs are conducted in a pRHL-like logic, where
successive sampling statements can be swapped thanks to the commutativity of the monad.

SSProve
(
Abate et al. 2021
;
Haselwarter et al. 2021
)
supports modular crypto proofs by composing “packages” of programs written in the free monad for state and probabilities.
The swap rule in SSProve allows exchanging commands which maintain a state invariant. Reasoning about dynamically allocated local state is not supported.

IPDL
(
Gancher et al. 2023
)
is a process calculus for stating and proving cryptographic observational equivalences.
IPDL is mechanized in Coq and targeted at equational reasoning about interactive message-passing in high-level cryptographic protocol models, and hence considers a different set of language features.

10.
Conclusion

We have presented Clutch, a novel higher-order probabilistic relational separation logic with support for asynchronous probabilistic coupling-based proofs of contextual refinement and equivalence of probabilistic higher-order programs with local state and impredicative polymorphism.
We have proved the soundness of Clutch formally in Coq using a range of new technical concepts and ideas such as
left-partial couplings
,
presampling tapes
, and a
coupling modality
.
We have demonstrated the usefulness of our approach through several example program equivalences that, to the best of our knowledge, were not possible to establish with previous methods.

Data Availability Statement

The Coq formalization accompanying this work is available on Zenodo
(
Gregersen et al. 2023
)
and on GitHub at
https://github.com/logsem/clutch
.

Foundation.
This work was co-funded by the European Union (ERC, CHORDS, 101096090).
Views and opinions expressed are however those of the author(s) only and do not necessarily reflect
those of the European Union or the European Research Council. Neither the European Union nor
the granting authority can be held responsible for them.
This material is based upon work supported in part by the Sponsor National Science Foundation
under Grant No. Grant #2318724.

.

\displaystyle{}\qquad(v_{1}=\operatorname{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{inr}}}(w_{1})\mskip 5.0mu{\ast}\mskip 5.0muv_{2}=\operatorname{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{inr}}}(w_{2})\mskip 5.0mu{\ast}\mskip 5.0mu\llbracket\sigma\rrbracket_{\Delta}(w_{1},w_{2}))

\displaystyle\llbracket\mu\,\alpha.\penalty\thinspace\tau\rrbracket_{\Delta}(v_{1},v_{2})

\displaystyle\triangleq\left(\mu R.\penalty\thinspace\lambda(v_{1},v_{2}).\penalty\thinspace\exists w_{1},w_{2}.\penalty\thinspace(v_{1}=\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{fold}}\penalty\thinspace w_{1})\mskip 5.0mu{\ast}\mskip 5.0mu(v^{\prime}=\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{fold}}\penalty\thinspace w_{2})\mskip 5.0mu{\ast}\mskip 5.0mu\mathop{{\triangleright}}\llbracket\tau\rrbracket_{\Delta,\alpha\mapsto R}(w_{1},w_{2})\right)(v_{1},v_{2})

\displaystyle\llbracket\forall\alpha.\penalty\thinspace\tau\rrbracket_{\Delta}(v_{1},v_{2})

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}}}\mskip 1.66667mu\mathord{\mathrel{{{\mid\kern-1.72218pt\Rrightarrow\kern-1.07639pt}}_{\emptyset}}\kern 0.86108pt}\Psi((e_{1},\sigma_{2}),({e_{1}}^{\prime},{\sigma_{2}}^{\prime}))\right)

\frac{\mathsf{red}(\rho_{1})\quad\stepdistr(\rho_{1})\sim\stepdistr(\rho_{1}^{\prime}):R\quad\forall\rho_{2},{\rho_{2}}^{\prime}.\penalty\thinspace R(\rho_{2},{\rho_{2}}^{\prime})\mskip 1.66667mu\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\hbox to10.29pt{\vbox to4.65pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25833pt\lower-2.32639pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}

Appendix B
On Case Studies and Additional Examples

B.1.
Sangiorgi and Vignudelli’s “copying” example

Sangiorgi and Vignudelli prove a subtle contextual equivalence mixing probabilistic choice, local references, and recursion using environmental bisimulations

(
Sangiorgi and Vignudelli 2016
)
.
Under call-by-value evaluation,
\lambda
-abstraction fails to distribute over probabilistic choice. This is contrary to call-by-name, and can easily be seen by considering the terms
I
and
L
in
Figure

15
. When evaluated in context
(\lambda f.\penalty\thinspace f()=f())[\,\cdot\,]
,
I
returns
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{true}}
(and
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{false}}
) with probability
\frac{1}{2}
, while
L
returns
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{true}}
with probability
1
.
The non-linear use of
f
in the context is characteristic of examples that behave differently under call-by-name and call-by-value. The equivalence of
K
and
H
is achieved by prohibiting such a “copying” use by exploiting local state.

The environmental bisimulation technique developed in
(
Sangiorgi and Vignudelli 2016
)
is sufficiently powerful to prove the equivalence as it works directly with the resulting distributions, but, to our knowledge, previous attempts at a proof working abstractly with programs via logical relations were not successful
(
Bizjak 2016
, Sec. 1.5)
.

Intuitively,
K
and
H
should be equivalent despite the fact that abstraction does not distribute over probabilistic choice because the closures they return are protected by a counter that only allows them to be run once. On the first call, both have equal probability of returning
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{true}}
or
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{false}}
. On subsequent calls, the counter
x
ensures that they both diverge.

\displaystyle L\triangleq{}

\displaystyle(\lambda\_.\penalty\thinspace\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{true}})\oplus(\lambda\_.\penalty\thinspace\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{false}})

\displaystyle I\triangleq{}

\displaystyle\lambda\_.\penalty\thinspace(\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{true}}\oplus\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{false}})

\displaystyle K\triangleq{}

\displaystyle\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{let}}\penalty\thinspace x\mathrel{=}\operatorname{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{ref}}}0\penalty\thinspace\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{in}}\penalty\thinspace(\lambda\_.\penalty\thinspace M)\oplus(\lambda\_.\penalty\thinspace N)

\displaystyle H\triangleq{}

\displaystyle\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{let}}\penalty\thinspace x\mathrel{=}\operatorname{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{ref}}}0\penalty\thinspace\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{in}}\penalty\thinspace\lambda\_.\penalty\thinspace(M\oplus N)

\displaystyle H_{\iota}\triangleq{}

\displaystyle\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{let}}\penalty\thinspace x\mathrel{=}\operatorname{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{ref}}}0\penalty\thinspace\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{in}}\penalty\thinspace

\displaystyle\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{let}}\penalty\thinspace\iota\mathrel{=}\operatorname{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{tape}}}1\penalty\thinspace\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{in}}\penalty\thinspace

\displaystyle\lambda\_.\penalty\thinspace(M\oplus_{\iota}N)

\displaystyle M\triangleq{}

\displaystyle\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{if}}\penalty\thinspace\mathop{!}x=0\penalty\thinspace\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{then}}\penalty\thinspace x\leftarrow 1;\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{true}}\penalty\thinspace\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{else}}\penalty\thinspace\Omega

\displaystyle N\triangleq{}

\displaystyle\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{if}}\penalty\thinspace\mathop{!}x=0\penalty\thinspace\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{then}}\penalty\thinspace x\leftarrow 1;\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{false}}\penalty\thinspace\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{else}}\penalty\thinspace\Omega

\displaystyle\Omega\triangleq{}

\displaystyle(\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{rec}}\penalty\thinspace f\penalty\thinspace x\mathrel{=}fx)()

\displaystyle e_{1}\oplus\phantom{{}_{\iota}}e_{2}\triangleq{}

\displaystyle\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{if}}\penalty\thinspace\operatorname{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{flip}}}()\penalty\thinspace\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{then}}\penalty\thinspace e_{1}\penalty\thinspace\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{else}}\penalty\thinspace e_{2}

\displaystyle e_{1}\oplus_{\iota}e_{2}\triangleq{}

\displaystyle\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{if}}\penalty\thinspace\operatorname{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{flip}}}(\iota)\penalty\thinspace\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{then}}\penalty\thinspace e_{1}\penalty\thinspace\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{else}}\penalty\thinspace e_{2}

Figure 15
.
Sangiorgi and Vignudelli 2016
’s example.

The key insight that allows us to prove
K
and
H
contextually equivalent in Clutch is to establish an asynchronous coupling between the two
\operatorname{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{flip}}}
operations.
Similarly to the proof of the lazy/eager coin example, we employ an intermediary version
H_{\iota}
of the program
H
in which the sampling is delayed until the closure is run. The equivalence of
H_{\iota}
and
H
follows from
22
and the symbolic execution rules.

The refinement
\emptyset\vDash_{\top}H_{\iota}\precsim K:\mathsf{unit}\to\mathsf{bool}
is established by allocating a tape
\iota
and coupling the (eager)
\operatorname{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{flip}}}()
in
K
with the tape
\iota
.
Because allocation of
\iota
is local to
H_{\iota}
, we obtain exclusive ownership of the tape resource
\iota\hookrightarrow(1,\epsilon)
.
In particular, other parts of the program,
i.e.
the context in which
H_{\iota}
is evaluated in, cannot sample to or consume bits from
\iota
.
By
24
, we resolve the
\operatorname{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{flip}}}()
to
b
in
K
and obtain
\iota\hookrightarrow(1,b)
for
H_{\iota}
.
We then allocate the non-atomic invariant:

\displaystyle(\iota\hookrightarrow(1,b)\mskip 5.0mu{\ast}\mskip 5.0mux\mapsto_{\mathsf{s}}0\mskip 5.0mu{\ast}\mskip 5.0mux\mapsto 0)\lor(\iota\hookrightarrow(1,\epsilon)\mskip 5.0mu{\ast}\mskip 5.0mux\mapsto_{\mathsf{s}}1\mskip 5.0mu{\ast}\mskip 5.0mux\mapsto 1)

The invariant describes the two possible states of the programs. Either the closures returned by
K
and
H_{\iota}
have not been run yet, in which case the presampled bit
b
is still on tape
\iota
and the counter
x
is
0
in both programs, or the bit has been consumed, and the counter is
1
in both programs.
It is worth noting here that we will rely crucially on a form of local state encapsulation for tapes, which guarantees that once
b
has been read from
\iota
, the tape remains empty. We only consider the case where
b=\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{true}}
; the other case is analogous.

With the invariant in hand, we apply the proof rule for functions to work on the bodies of the two closures.
As a first step, we open our invariant, and are left to prove the equivalence in both cases of the disjunction.
By virtue of the non-atomic nature of the invariant, we can keep it open for several steps of evaluation, involving
\operatorname{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{flip}}}
, pure reductions, and state-manipulating operations, until it is finally reestablished.

In the first case, we read
b
from
\iota
, yielding
\iota\hookrightarrow(1,\epsilon)
. We are left to prove the refinement of two structurally equal programs:

\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{if}}\penalty\thinspace\mathop{!}x=0\penalty\thinspace\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{then}}\penalty\thinspace x\leftarrow 1;\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{true}}\penalty\thinspace\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{else}}\penalty\thinspace\Omega

We take the first branch and set
x
to
1
. We have now reproven the invariant, and both programs return
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{true}}
and conclude.

In the second case of the invariant, the closures have been invoked before, and we expect them to both diverge. However, before evaluation reaches
\Omega
in
H_{\iota}
, another
\operatorname{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{flip}}}(\iota)
has to be resolved.
Here we exploit the fact that the
\iota
tape remains empty once we read
b
, as it is local to
H_{\iota}
.
Logically, this observation manifests in the fact that after the allocation of
\iota
, its ownership has been transferred into the invariant, and is now reclaimed.
We can thus use
23
to resolve the
\operatorname{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{flip}}}
on an empty tape to a new random bit
b^{\prime}
.
Irrespectively of the value of
b^{\prime}
, both programs diverge because we know that
x\mapsto_{\mathsf{s}}1
and
x\mapsto 1
.
A diverging term refines any other term; in particular we appeal to
21
to conclude the proof.

B.2.
ElGamal security proof

\mathit{PK}\mathit{{}_{rand}}

\simeq_{\text{ctx}}

\displaystyle\smash{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{let}}\penalty\thinspace sk\mathrel{=}\operatorname{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{rand}}}(n)\penalty\thinspace\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{in}}\penalty\thinspace}

\displaystyle\smash{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{let}}\penalty\thinspace pk\mathrel{=}g^{sk}\penalty\thinspace\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{in}}\penalty\thinspace}

\displaystyle\smash{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{let}}\penalty\thinspace\mathit{query}=\lambda\ msg.\penalty\thinspace}

\displaystyle\smash{\quad\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{if}}\penalty\thinspace\mathop{!}{count}\neq 0\penalty\thinspace\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{then}}\penalty\thinspace}

\displaystyle\smash{\quad\quad\operatorname{\mathsf{None}}}

\displaystyle\smash{\quad\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{else}}\penalty\thinspace}

\displaystyle\smash{\quad\quad count\leftarrow 1;}

\displaystyle\smash{\quad\quad\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{let}}\penalty\thinspace b\mathrel{=}\operatorname{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{rand}}}(n)\penalty\thinspace\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{in}}\penalty\thinspace}

\displaystyle\smash{\quad\quad\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{let}}\penalty\thinspace x\mathrel{=}\operatorname{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{rand}}}(n)\penalty\thinspace\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{in}}\penalty\thinspace}

\displaystyle\smash{\quad\quad\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{let}}\penalty\thinspace(B,X)\mathrel{=}(g^{b},g^{x})\penalty\thinspace\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{in}}\penalty\thinspace}

\displaystyle\smash{\quad\quad\operatorname{\mathsf{Some}}\ (B,X)}

\displaystyle\smash{\penalty\thinspace\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{in}}\penalty\thinspace(pk,\mathit{query})}

\mathcal{C}[\mathit{DH_{rand}}]

\displaystyle\smash{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{let}}\penalty\thinspace(pk,\text{\lx@texthl@color{$B,C$}})=}

\displaystyle\smash{\quad\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{let}}\penalty\thinspace a\mathrel{=}\operatorname{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{rand}}}(n)\penalty\thinspace\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{in}}\penalty\thinspace}

\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{let}}\penalty\thinspace b\mathrel{=}\operatorname{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{rand}}}(n)\penalty\thinspace\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{in}}\penalty\thinspace
