\displaystyle\smash{\quad(g^{a},\text{\lx@texthl@color{$g^{b},g^{c}$}})\penalty\thinspace\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{in}}\penalty\thinspace}

\displaystyle\smash{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{let}}\penalty\thinspace count\mathrel{=}\operatorname{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{ref}}}0\penalty\thinspace\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{in}}\penalty\thinspace}

\displaystyle\smash{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{let}}\penalty\thinspace\mathit{query}=\lambda\ msg.\penalty\thinspace}

\displaystyle\smash{\quad\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{if}}\penalty\thinspace\mathop{!}{count}\neq 0\penalty\thinspace\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{then}}\penalty\thinspace}

\displaystyle\smash{\quad\quad\operatorname{\mathsf{None}}}

\displaystyle\smash{\quad\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{else}}\penalty\thinspace}

\displaystyle\smash{\quad\quad count\leftarrow 1;}

\displaystyle\smash{\quad\quad\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{let}}\penalty\thinspace X\mathrel{=}msg\cdot{}C\penalty\thinspace\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{in}}\penalty\thinspace}

\displaystyle\smash{\quad\quad\operatorname{\mathsf{Some}}\ (B,X)}

\displaystyle\smash{\penalty\thinspace\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{in}}\penalty\thinspace(pk,\mathit{query})}

Figure 16
.
The “rand” direction of the security reduction.

As stated in the main paper, the proof of
\vdash\mathit{PK}\mathit{{}_{rand}}\simeq_{\text{ctx}}\mathcal{C}[\mathit{DH_{rand}}]:\tau{}_{\mathit{\mathit{PK}}}
is similar to that of
\vdash\mathit{PK}\mathit{{}_{real}}\simeq_{\text{ctx}}\mathcal{C}[\mathit{DH_{real}}]:\tau{}_{\mathit{\mathit{PK}}}
.
The sequence of games is displayed in
Figure

16
.
Compared to
\mathit{PK}\mathit{{}_{rand}}
, the
\mathit{PK}^{\mathit{tape}}_{\mathit{rand}}
game samples from tapes
\beta
and
\gamma
.
This difference is immaterial, but the tapes will be used to connect the lazily-sampling
\mathit{PK}^{\mathit{tape}}_{\mathit{rand}}
to the eagerly-sampling
\mathcal{C}[\mathit{DH_{rand}}]
.
More interestingly, the
X
component of the ciphertext is computed directly as a random group element
g^{x}
in
\mathit{PK}\mathit{{}_{rand}}
, whereas
\mathit{PK}^{\mathit{tape}}_{\mathit{rand}}
multiplies the message
msg
with a random group element
C
.
We have to justify that the two games are nonetheless equivalent.
Intuitively speaking, since
C
is uniformly distributed, so is
msg\cdot C
, and hence
X
is a group element sampled from a uniform distribution in both games.
The rigorous explanation for this argument hinges on the fact that multiplication with
msg
induces a bijection on the set
\{0,1,\ldots,n\}
.

Recall that the group
G
is of order
n+1
.
Since
G
is generated by
g
, we can thus write
msg=g^{k}
for some
k\in\{0,1,\ldots,n\}
.
We can therefore regroup the exponents in
msg\cdot{}C=g^{k}\cdot{}C=g^{k}\cdot g^{c}
as
g^{k+c}
.
Since the function
f\triangleq\lambda x.\penalty\thinspace(x-k)\bmod(n+1)
is a bijection on
\{0,1,\ldots,n\}
, we can couple the sampling of
x
in
\mathit{PK}\mathit{{}_{rand}}
with the sampling of
c
in
\mathit{PK}^{\mathit{tape}}_{\mathit{rand}}
to obtain some value
r
for
x
in
\mathit{PK}\mathit{{}_{rand}}
and
f(r)
for
c
in
\mathit{PK}^{\mathit{tape}}_{\mathit{rand}}
.
By definition of
f
, the value computed for
X
in
\mathit{PK}^{\mathit{tape}}_{\mathit{rand}}
is then equal to
X=msg\cdot{}C=g^{k+c}=g^{k+f(r)}=g^{k+r-k}=g^{r}
, just as in
\mathit{PK}\mathit{{}_{rand}}
, and the two programs are equivalent.
This argument is a standard ingredient in the security proof of ElGamal, and part of our formalization.

The main difference between
\mathit{PK}^{\mathit{tape}}_{\mathit{rand}}
and
\mathcal{C}[\mathit{DH_{rand}}]
is that the
\mathit{query}
function in
\mathit{PK}^{\mathit{tape}}_{\mathit{rand}}
samples
b
and
c
lazily, whereas in
\mathcal{C}[\mathit{DH_{rand}}]
, the sampling of
b
and
c
occurs eagerly in the beginning.
Once again, the proof proceeds in a manner similar to the lazy-eager coin example.

B.3.
Eager/Lazy Hash Function

As explained in the body of the paper, it is
common to model the hash function as if it satisfies the so-called

uniform hash assumption
or the
random oracle model
. That is, a hash function
h
from a
set of keys
K
to values
V
behaves as if, for each key
k
, the
hash
h(k)
is randomly sampled from a uniform distribution over
V
,
independently of all the other keys.

\displaystyle\mathit{sample\_all}\triangleq{}

\displaystyle\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{rec}}\penalty\thinspace f\penalty\thinspace m\penalty\thinspace n\mathrel{=}

\displaystyle\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{let}}\penalty\thinspace n^{\prime}\mathrel{=}n-1\penalty\thinspace\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{in}}\penalty\thinspace

\displaystyle\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{if}}\penalty\thinspace n^{\prime}<0\penalty\thinspace\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{then}}\penalty\thinspace()\penalty\thinspace\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{else}}\penalty\thinspace

\displaystyle\quad\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{let}}\penalty\thinspace b\mathrel{=}\operatorname{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{flip}}}()\penalty\thinspace\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{in}}\penalty\thinspace

\displaystyle\quad\mathit{set}\ m\ n^{\prime}\ b;

\displaystyle\quad f\ m\ n^{\prime}

\displaystyle\mathit{eager\_hash}\triangleq{}

\displaystyle\lambda n.\penalty\thinspace

\displaystyle\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{let}}\penalty\thinspace m\mathrel{=}\mathit{init\_map}\ ()\penalty\thinspace\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{in}}\penalty\thinspace

\displaystyle\mathit{sample\_all}\ m\ (n+1);

\displaystyle(\lambda k.\penalty\thinspace{\begin{array}[t]{rll}\lx@intercol\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{match}}\penalty\thinspace\mathit{get}\ m\ k\penalty\thinspace\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{with}}\hfil\lx@intercol\\
&\operatorname{\mathsf{Some}}(b)&\Rightarrow b\\
|&\operatorname{\mathsf{None}}&\Rightarrow\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{false}}\\
\lx@intercol\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{end}})\hfil\lx@intercol\end{array}}{}

Figure 17
.
Eager hash function.

Figure

17
gives the complete code for the eager
hash function that was excerpted earlier.
Given a non-negative integer
n
, executing
\mathit{eager\_hash}\ n
returns a hash
function with
K=\{0,\dots,n\}
and
V=\mathbb{B}
. To do so, it
first initializes a mutable map
m
, and then calls
\mathit{sample\_all}
on

m
. For each key
k\in K
, the function
\mathit{sample\_all}
samples a
boolean
b
with
\operatorname{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{flip}}}
and stores the value
b
for the key
k
in
the map
m
. This sampled boolean serves as the hash for
b
. The
function returned by
\mathit{eager\_hash}
uses this map to look up the hash
values of keys. On input
k
, it looks up
k
in the map and returns
the resulting value if one is found, and otherwise returns

\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{false}}
. Since
\mathit{sample\_all}
adds every key in
K
to the map, this
latter scenario only happens if
k
is not in
K
.

\displaystyle\mathit{alloc\_tapes}\triangleq{}

\displaystyle\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{rec}}\penalty\thinspace f\penalty\thinspace m\penalty\thinspace n\mathrel{=}

\displaystyle\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{let}}\penalty\thinspace n^{\prime}\mathrel{=}n-1\penalty\thinspace\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{in}}\penalty\thinspace

\displaystyle\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{if}}\penalty\thinspace n^{\prime}<0\penalty\thinspace\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{then}}\penalty\thinspace()\penalty\thinspace\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{else}}\penalty\thinspace

\displaystyle\quad\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{let}}\penalty\thinspace\iota\mathrel{=}\operatorname{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{tape}}}\penalty\thinspace\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{in}}\penalty\thinspace

\displaystyle\quad\mathit{set}\ m\ n^{\prime}\ \iota;

\displaystyle\quad f\ m\ n^{\prime}

\displaystyle\mathit{lazy\_hash}\triangleq{}

\displaystyle\lambda n.\penalty\thinspace

\displaystyle\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{let}}\penalty\thinspace vm\mathrel{=}\mathit{init\_map}\ ()\penalty\thinspace\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{in}}\penalty\thinspace

\displaystyle\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{let}}\penalty\thinspace tm\mathrel{=}\mathit{init\_map}\ ()\penalty\thinspace\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{in}}\penalty\thinspace

\displaystyle\mathit{alloc\_tapes}\ tm\ (n+1);

\displaystyle(\lambda k.\penalty\thinspace{\begin{array}[t]{rll}\lx@intercol\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{match}}\penalty\thinspace\mathit{get}\ vm\ k\penalty\thinspace\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{with}}\hfil\lx@intercol\\
&\operatorname{\mathsf{Some}}(b)\Rightarrow\\
&\quad b\\
|&\operatorname{\mathsf{None}}\Rightarrow{{\begin{array}[t]{rll}\lx@intercol\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{match}}\penalty\thinspace\mathit{get}\ tm\ k\penalty\thinspace\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{with}}\hfil\lx@intercol\\
&\operatorname{\mathsf{Some}}(\iota)\Rightarrow\\
&\quad{\begin{array}[t]{l}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{let}}\penalty\thinspace b\mathrel{=}\operatorname{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{flip}}}(\iota)\penalty\thinspace\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{in}}\\
\mathit{set}\ vm\ b;\\
b\end{array}}\\
|&\operatorname{\mathsf{None}}\Rightarrow\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{false}}\\
\lx@intercol\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{end}}\hfil\lx@intercol\end{array}}}\\
\lx@intercol\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{end}})\hfil\lx@intercol\end{array}}{}

Figure 18
.
Lazy hash function.

Figure

18
gives the full code for the lazy sampling version of the random hash generator.
Given a non-negative integer
n
, executing
\mathit{lazy\_hash}\ n
returns a
hash function for the key space
K=\{0,\dots,n\}
. For its
internal state, it uses two physical maps, the tape map
tm
, stores
tapes to be used for random sampling, and the value map
vm
, stores
the previously sampled values for keys that have been hashed. After
initializing these maps, it calls
\mathit{alloc\_tapes}
, which allocates a
tape for each key
k\in K
and stores the associated tape in
tm
.
The hash function returned by
\mathit{lazy\_hash}
determines the hash for a
key
k
in two stages. It first looks up
k
in
vm
to see if
k

already has a previously sampled hash value, and if so, returns the
found value. Otherwise, it looks up
k
in the tape map
tm
. If no
tape is found, then
k
must not be in
K
, so the function returns

\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{false}}
. If a tape
\iota
is found, then the code samples a boolean

b
from this tape with
\operatorname{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{flip}}}
, stores
b
for the key
k
in
vm
,
and then returns
b
.

We
also prove that for any non-negative number
n
, the eager and lazy
versions are contextually equivalent, that is,

\vdash\mathit{eager\_hash}\ n\simeq_{\text{ctx}}\mathit{lazy\_hash}\ n:\mathsf{int}\rightarrow\mathsf{bool}
.
The core idea behind this contextual equivalence proof is to maintain
a particular invariant between the internal state of the two hash functions.
Let
m
be the internal map used by the eager hash and let
tm
and
vm
be the tape
and value maps, respectively, for the lazy hash. Then, at a high level, the invariant
maintains the following properties:

(1)

\mathsf{dom}(m)=\mathsf{dom}(tm)=\{0,\dots,n\}
.

(2)

For all
k\in\{0,\dots,n\}
, if
m[k]=b
then either

(a)

vm[k]=b
, or

(b)

vm[k]=\bot
and
tm[k]=\iota
for some tape label
\iota
such that
\iota\hookrightarrow([b],
).

Case (a) and (b) of the second part of this invariant capture the two possible states each key
k
can be in.
Either hash of
k
has been looked up before (case a), and so the sampled value stored in
vm
must match that of
m
, or it has not been looked up (case b) and the tape for the key must contain the same value as
m[k]
for its next bit.

To establish this invariant when the hashes are initialized, we
asynchronously couple the eager hash function’s
\operatorname{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{flip}}}
for key
k

with a tape step for the tape
\iota
associated with
k
in the lazy
table. The invariant ensures that the values returned by the two hash
functions will be the same when a key
k
is queried. The cases of
the invariant corresponding to the branches of the lazy function’s
match statements: if the key
k
is in
K
and has been queried
before, the maps will return the same values found in
m
and
vm
. If
it has not been queried before, then the
\operatorname{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{flip}}}
statement in the lazy
version will be draw the value on the tape for the key, which matches

m[k]
. Moreover, the update that writes this sampled value to
vm

preserves the invariant, switching from case (b) to case (a) for
queried key.

B.4.
Random Generators from Hashes

Here we provide further details on generating a random boolean sampler from a lazy hash function.
The following function,
\mathit{init\_hash\_rng}
, returns
a function that can be used to generate random booleans:

\displaystyle\mathit{init\_hash\_rng}\triangleq{}

\displaystyle\lambda\_.\penalty\thinspace

\displaystyle\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{let}}\penalty\thinspace f\mathrel{=}\mathit{lazy\_hash}\ \texttt{MAX}\penalty\thinspace\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{in}}\penalty\thinspace

\displaystyle\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{let}}\penalty\thinspace c\mathrel{=}\operatorname{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{ref}}}{0}\penalty\thinspace\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{in}}\penalty\thinspace

\displaystyle(\lambda\_.\penalty\thinspace\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{let}}\penalty\thinspace n\mathrel{=}\mathop{!}{c}\penalty\thinspace\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{in}}\penalty\thinspace

\displaystyle\quad\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{let}}\penalty\thinspace b\mathrel{=}f\ n\penalty\thinspace\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{in}}\penalty\thinspace

\displaystyle\quad c\leftarrow n+1;

\displaystyle\quad b)

When run,
\mathit{init\_hash\_rng}
generates a lazy hash function
f
for the key space
K=\{0,\dots,\texttt{MAX}\}

for some fixed constant
MAX
. It then allocates a counter
c
as a mutable reference initialized to
0
.
The returned function, let us call it
h
, uses
f
and
c
to generate random booleans. Each time

h
is called, it loads the current value
n
form
c
, hashes
n
with
f
to get
a boolean
b
. It then increments
c
and returns the boolean
b
, which serves as a random boolean.
Repeated calls to
h
return independent, uniformly sampled booleans, so long as we make no more than
MAX
calls.
The reason this works is that we have assumed the hash function
f
is uniformly random, so the hashes of different keys are independently sampled. So long as we make fewer than
MAX
calls to
h
, each call will hash a distinct number
n
(the current counter value), so it will be independent of all previous and future calls. After
MAX
calls,
c
will exceed
MAX
and so we will hash a key outside of
f
’s key space. Recall from the previous example that
\mathit{lazy\_hash}
returns
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{false}}
on inputs outside its key space.

This example might at first seem artificial, but using cryptographic
primitives such as hashes or block ciphers to generate pseudorandom
numbers is in fact commonly done
(
Barker and Kelsey 2015
)
. Although the example
here is simplified compared to real implementations, it captures one
of the core verification challenges common to real implementations.
Namely, for correctness, one must show that the “key” or “counter” being
hashed or encrypted (here the values of
n
obtained from
c
) are not
re-used.

To capture the guarantees of
\mathit{init\_hash\_rng}
more formally, we prove
that
\mathit{init\_hash\_rng}
is contextually equivalent to the following
“bounded” random number generator that directly calls
\operatorname{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{flip}}}
:

\displaystyle\mathit{init\_bounded\_rng}\triangleq{}

\displaystyle\qquad\lambda\_.\penalty\thinspace

\displaystyle\qquad\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{let}}\penalty\thinspace c\mathrel{=}\operatorname{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{ref}}}{0}\penalty\thinspace\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{in}}\penalty\thinspace

\displaystyle\qquad(\lambda\_.\penalty\thinspace\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{let}}\penalty\thinspace n\mathrel{=}\mathop{!}{c}\penalty\thinspace\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{in}}\penalty\thinspace

\displaystyle\qquad\quad\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{let}}\penalty\thinspace b\mathrel{=}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{if}}\penalty\thinspace n\leq\texttt{MAX}\penalty\thinspace\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{then}}\penalty\thinspace\operatorname{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{flip}}}()\penalty\thinspace\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{else}}\penalty\thinspace\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{false}}\penalty\thinspace\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{in}}\penalty\thinspace

\displaystyle\qquad\quad c\leftarrow n+1;

\displaystyle\qquad\quad b)

With
\mathit{init\_bounded\_rng}
, the returned generator function again uses a
counter
c
, however the value of this counter is just used to track
the number of samples generated. If the number of calls is less than or equal to
MAX
,
it returns a boolean generated by a call to
\operatorname{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{flip}}}
. Otherwise,
it just returns
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{false}}
. (In
\mathbf{F}_{\mu,\textnormal{ref}}^{\textnormal{rand}}
, integers are unbounded, so there is no
issue with overflow.)

At a high level, the proof proceeds by maintaining the following
invariant relating the generator functions returned by both

\mathit{init\_hash\_rng}
and
\mathit{init\_bounded\_rng}
. Let
f
be the hash function in the hash-based generator,
n_{h}
be its counter value, and
n_{b}
be the value of the bounded generator’s counter. Then:

(1)

n_{h}=n_{b}
.

(2)

The key space of
f
is
\{0,\dots,\texttt{MAX}\}

(3)

For all
k\in\{0,\dots,\texttt{MAX}\}
, if
k\geq n_{h}
then
f
has not yet hashed
k
.

The first and second parts of the invariant guarantees that once
n_{h}

exceeds
MAX
, and thus falls outside
f
’s key space, both
generators will return the same value of
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{false}}
. In addition, based on the third part of
the invariant, so long as
n_{h}\in\{0,\dots,\texttt{MAX}\}

when the hash-based generator evaluates
f\ n_{h}
, we will be able
to couple the hash value it samples with the
\operatorname{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{flip}}}
command in the bounded generator, ensuring
that both generators return the same value.

The
\mathit{init\_hash\_rng}
function above generates a single random generator
from a hash function. However, in some scenarios, it is necessary to
be able to generate multiple independent streams of random
numbers. For example, in a language with parallelism or concurrency,
using a single generator returned by
\mathit{init\_hash\_rng}
in multiple
threads would mean sharing mutable access to the counter
c
, so that
synchronization primitives would be needed to prevent racy accesses.
Related issues have motivated the need to “split” a random number generator
into two streams in the context of a lazy language like Haskell
(
Claessen and Palka 2013
)
.

While
\mathbf{F}_{\mu,\textnormal{ref}}^{\textnormal{rand}}
is sequential, we can still explore the question of
how to create multiple independent random generators. One approach
would be to call
\mathit{init\_hash\_rng}
multiple times. But that assumes that
we have the ability to initialize multiple random oracle hash
functions. In practice, if we instantiate the random oracle model with
a particular concrete hash function, like SHA-256, we cannot feasibly
use different hash functions each time
\mathit{init\_hash\_rng}
is
called. Instead, we would like a way to generate multiple independent
random number generators from a single hash function.

The solution is to get the illusion of multiple independent hash functions out of a single hash function by partitioning the key space of the hash. Specifically, we use a wrapper around the hash function so that it now takes
two
integers as input, instead of one, to obtain a so-called
keyed
hash
8
8

8

In cryptographic settings, a similar construction is called a hash message authentication code (HMAC). However, HMACs typically use a different way of combining the two arguments to avoid certain vulnerabilities.
:

\displaystyle\mathit{lazy\_keyed\_hash}\triangleq{}

\displaystyle\lambda\_.\penalty\thinspace

\displaystyle\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{let}}\penalty\thinspace f\mathrel{=}\mathit{lazy\_hash}\ (2^{(p_{k}+p_{v})}-1)\penalty\thinspace\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{in}}\penalty\thinspace

\displaystyle(\lambda k\ v.\penalty\thinspace f\ (k\cdot 2^{p_{v}}+v))

The returned keyed hash function takes two inputs, a key
k
and a
value
v
to be hashed, combines them into a single integer, and calls
the lazy hash function
f
on that single integer. The values
p_{k}

and
p_{v}
are fixed constants that determine the range of the keys and
values that can be hashed. If
h
is the returned hash function, we
can treat the partially-applied functions
h\ k_{1}
and
h\ k_{2}
for

k_{1}\neq k_{2}
as if they were two independent hash functions, so long
as we do not apply the functions to values
v
that are larger than

2^{p_{v}}-1
.

Using this, we have verified a version of the hash-based random number
generator that supports splitting multiple independent generators out
of a single hash. Each generator has a distinct key
k
its own
internal counter
c
. When a sample is requested, it reads the value

n
from the counter and computes the keyed hash of
k
and
n
to get
a boolean. To ensure that the keys used by the generators are
distinct, we assign keys using a shared counter that is incremented
every time a new generator is initialized. The complete details can be
found in the accompanying Coq development.

B.5.
Lazily Sampled Big Integers

Our last example is motivated by a data structure called a
treap
(
Seidel and Aragon 1996
)
. A treap is a binary search tree
structure that relies on randomization to ensure with high probability
that the tree will be balanced. One of the key aspects of the treap
is that every key that is inserted into the treap is first assigned a
random numerical
priority
. During the insertion process, this
priority value is compared with the priorities of keys already in the
treap. The exact details of this comparison process are not relevant
here; what is important to know is that, ideally, all of the assigned
priority values are different (that is, there are no
collisions

of priorities). Thus, in analyzing the treap, it is common to treat
these priorities as if they are sampled from a continuous
distribution, such as the uniform distribution on the interval

[0,1]
, to ensure that the probability of collisions is 0.
Eberl et al.
(
Eberl et al. 2020
)
have previously mechanized such an analysis
of treaps in Isabelle/HOL.

In actual implementations, the priorities are instead typically
represented with some fixed precision, say as an approximate floating
point number sampled from
[0,1]
, or as an integer sampled uniformly
from some set
\{0,\dots,n\}
, so that there is some probability of
collision. However, in the latter case, as long as
n
is big enough
relative to the number of keys added to the tree, the probability of a
collision can be kept low, and the performance properties of the treap
are preserved. The probability of a collision is an instance of the
well-known “birthday problem”.

But in some scenarios, we may need to decide on
n
without knowing in
advance how many keys will end up being added to the treap. If we err
on the conservative side by making
n
very large, say
2^{256}-1
,
the probability of a collision will be very low, but we will need to
use
256
bits to store the priorities, which is wasteful if we end up
only storing a moderate number of nodes.

An alternative is to
lazily
sample the integer that represents
the priority. The insight is that the actual numerical value of the priorities
is not relevant: the only operation that they must support is comparing two priorities
to determine if they are equal, and if not, which one is larger.

Figure

19
gives an implementation of a lazily-sampled integer
A lazily-sampled integer is encoded as a pair of a tape label
\iota
and a linked list of length at most
N
,
where each node in the list represents a
digit
of the integer in
base
B
, with the head of the list being the most significant digit.
For concreteness, here we consider
N=8
and
B=2^{32}
, so that the encoded numbers
can be at most
2^{256}-1
. Rather than sampling all digits up front, we instead
only sample digits when needed as part of comparing a lazy integer to another.

\displaystyle\mathit{sample\_lazy\_int}\triangleq{}

\displaystyle\lambda\_.\penalty\thinspace(\operatorname{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{tape}}},\operatorname{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{ref}}}\operatorname{\mathsf{None}})

\displaystyle\mathit{get\_next}\triangleq{}

\displaystyle\lambda\ \iota\ r.\penalty\thinspace

\displaystyle{\begin{array}[t]{rll}\lx@intercol\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{match}}\penalty\thinspace\mathop{!}{r}\penalty\thinspace\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{with}}\hfil\lx@intercol\\
&\operatorname{\mathsf{Some}}\ v&\Rightarrow v\\
|&\operatorname{\mathsf{None}}&\Rightarrow{\begin{array}[t]{l}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{let}}\penalty\thinspace z\mathrel{=}\mathit{sample32}\ \iota\penalty\thinspace\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{in}}\\
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{let}}\penalty\thinspace next\mathrel{=}\operatorname{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{ref}}}\operatorname{\mathsf{None}}\penalty\thinspace\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{in}}\\
r\leftarrow\operatorname{\mathsf{Some}}\ (z,next);\\
(z,next)\end{array}}\\
\lx@intercol\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{end}}\hfil\lx@intercol\end{array}}

\displaystyle\mathit{cmp\_list}\triangleq{}

\displaystyle\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{rec}}\penalty\thinspace f\penalty\thinspace n\ \iota_{1}\ l_{1}\ \iota_{2}\ l_{2}\mathrel{=}

\displaystyle\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{if}}\penalty\thinspace n=0\penalty\thinspace\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{then}}\penalty\thinspace 0

\displaystyle\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{else}}\penalty\thinspace

\displaystyle\quad\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{let}}\penalty\thinspace(z_{1},l_{1}^{\prime})\mathrel{=}\mathit{get\_next}\penalty\thinspace\iota_{1}\penalty\thinspace l_{1}\penalty\thinspace\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{in}}\penalty\thinspace

\displaystyle\quad\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{let}}\penalty\thinspace(z_{2},l_{2}^{\prime})\mathrel{=}\mathit{get\_next}\penalty\thinspace\iota_{2}\penalty\thinspace l_{2}\penalty\thinspace\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{in}}\penalty\thinspace

\displaystyle\quad\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{let}}\penalty\thinspace res\mathrel{=}\mathit{cmp}z_{1}\penalty\thinspace z_{2}\penalty\thinspace\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{in}}\penalty\thinspace

\displaystyle\quad\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{if}}\penalty\thinspace res=0\penalty\thinspace\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{then}}\penalty\thinspace

\displaystyle\quad\quad f\ (n-1)\ \iota_{1}\ l_{1}^{\prime}\ \iota_{2}\ l_{2}^{\prime}

\displaystyle\quad\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{else}}\penalty\thinspace\ res

\displaystyle\mathit{cmp\_lazy}\triangleq{}

\displaystyle\lambda\ (x_{1},x_{2}).\penalty\thinspace

\displaystyle\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{let}}\penalty\thinspace(\iota_{1},l_{1})\mathrel{=}x_{1}\penalty\thinspace\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{in}}\penalty\thinspace

\displaystyle\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{let}}\penalty\thinspace(\iota_{2},l_{2})\mathrel{=}x_{2}\penalty\thinspace\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{in}}\penalty\thinspace

\displaystyle\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{if}}\penalty\thinspace l_{1}=l_{2}\penalty\thinspace\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{then}}\penalty\thinspace 0

\displaystyle\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{else}}\penalty\thinspace\mathit{cmp\_list}\ 8\ \iota_{1}\ l_{1}\ \iota_{2}\ l_{2}

Figure 19
.
Implementation of lazily-sampled integers.

The function
\mathit{sample\_lazy\_int}
samples a lazy integer by generating a tape
and a reference to an empty linked list. At this point, the sampled
integer is entirely indeterminate. Given a tape label
\iota
and a
reference
r
to a digit in a lazy integer’s list, evaluating

\mathit{get\_next}\ \iota\ r
returns the integer
z
for that digit and a
reference
next
to the digit after
r
in the list. There are two
alternatives when getting the digit: either (1) the digit for
r
has
already been sampled, so that
\mathop{!}{r}
will be
\operatorname{\mathsf{Some}}\ v
, where
v

is a pair of the form
(z,next)
; or (2) the digit for
r
has not
yet been sampled, so that
\mathop{!}{r}
will be
\operatorname{\mathsf{None}}
. In case 1,

\mathit{get\_next}\ \iota\ r
just returns
v
. In case 2,
\mathit{get\_next}
will
first sample the value
z
for the digit by calling

\mathit{sample32}\ \iota
, which generates a 32 bit integer by sampling it
bit-by-bit with repeated calls to
\operatorname{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.0117,0.2695,0.457}{flip}}}\iota
. It then allocates a
new reference
next
for the next digit in the list, initialized to a
value
\operatorname{\mathsf{None}}
. Before returning
(z,next)
it stores this pair in
the reference
r
.

Evaluating
\mathit{cmp\_list}\ n\ \iota_{1}\ l_{1}\ \iota_{2}\ l_{2}
compares the two
lazy integers
(\iota_{1},l_{1})
and
(\iota_{2},l_{2})
by doing a
digit-by-digit comparison. It returns
-1
if the first integer is
smaller,
0
if the integers are equal, and
1
if the first integer
is larger. The first argument
n
tracks the number of remaining
digits in the integers. Let us consider the case that
n>0
first
(the else branch). In that case,
\mathit{cmp\_list}
will call
\mathit{get\_next}
on
each integer to get the next digit. Let
z_{1}
and
z_{2}
be these
digit values, respectively. These digits are compared using
\mathit{cmp}
,
which returns
-1
(if
z_{1}<z_{2}
),
0
(if
z_{1}=z_{2}
), or
1

(
z_{1}>z_{2}
). Because the lazy integers are stored with
most-significant digits earlier in the list, if
\mathit{cmp}\ z_{1}\ z_{2}
is
non-zero we already know which lazy integer is larger, and the result
of
\mathit{cmp}\ z_{1}\ z_{2}
gives the correct ordering of the whole lazy
integer. On the other hand if
\mathit{cmp}
returns
0
, then
z_{1}=z_{2}
,
in which case we cannot yet tell which lazy integer is larger. Thus,

\mathit{cmp\_list}
recursively calls itself to compare the next digits in the
lists, decrementing the
n
argument to track that there is one fewer
digit remaining. In the base case of the recursion, when
n=0
, that means all digits of the integers have been equal, hence
the value of the integers are equal, so we return
0
.
Because
\mathit{get\_next}
conveniently encapsulates the sampling of unsampled digits,
\mathit{cmp\_list}

looks like a normal traversal of the two linked lists, as if they were
eagerly sampled.

Note that if
x
is a lazy integer, then comparing
x
with itself
using
\mathit{cmp\_list}
unfortunately forces us to sample all of the
unsampled digits of
x
. The routine
\mathit{cmp\_lazy}
is a wrapper to

\mathit{cmp\_list}
that implements a small optimization to avoid this. The
function
\mathit{cmp\_lazy}
takes as input a pair of lazy integers
(x_{1},x_{2})
. Before calling
\mathit{cmp\_list}
, it first checks whether the pointers
to the heads of
x_{1}
and
x_{2}
’s lists are equal; if they are the two
integers must be equal, so it returns
0
immediately without calling

\mathit{cmp\_list}
.

We prove that this implementation of lazily-sampled integers is contextually
equivalent to code that eagerly samples an entire 256-bit integer by bit-shifting and adding
8 32-bit integers. This contextual equivalence is at an
abstract
existential type
\tau
. Specifically,
we define

\tau\triangleq\exists\alpha.\penalty\thinspace(\mathsf{unit}\rightarrow\alpha)\times((\alpha\times\alpha)\rightarrow\mathsf{int})

Then we have the following equivalence:

\vdash(\mathit{sample\_lazy\_int},\mathit{cmp\_lazy})\simeq_{\text{ctx}}(\mathit{sample256},\mathit{cmp}):\tau

The starting point for the proof is that when
\mathit{sample256}
samples
the 8 32-bit integers needed to assemble the 256-bit integer, we
couple these samples with identical samples on the tape
\iota

generated by
\mathit{sample\_lazy\_int}
. Then, the key invariant used in the proof
says that if we combine the digits of a lazy int that have already been
sampled, plus the remaining digits on the tape
\iota
, the result
represents an integer that is equivalent to the corresponding one
generated by
\mathit{sample256}
. This holds initially and is preserved by
calls to
\mathit{get\_next}
during
\mathit{cmp\_list}
, since it moves digits from the
tape to the linked list representing the integer.

Appendix C
Rules

We repeat the rules presented in the main paper for ease of reference.
